#10923. Nintendo is hiring someone to preserve its old games and consoles

Nintendo is looking to hire someone to help preserve its gaming history, as more and more classic titles risk being lost to time.

Video game preservation has become an increasingly important issue, especially as older consoles become obsolete and digital storefronts shut down. With many games never receiving modern re-releases, some are becoming virtually impossible to play without tracking down their original hardware.

In fact, a 2023 study found that 87% of classic games released in the US were no longer commercially available.

Now, Nintendo is taking steps to ensure that important parts of its own gaming history are preserved for future generations.

According to its official job listing, the company is searching for a Digital Archivist, who will help preserve technical records and development materials from previous generations.

“We are seeking a Digital Archivist, Historical Systems to support the preservation, organization, and accessibility of historical hardware and software information,” the description reads.

The successful applicant will be responsible for organizing historical documents, creating a searchable database, and interviewing long-serving employees to record their knowledge.

Nintendo says the goal is to ensure information about its older hardware and software remains accessible to its engineering, development, and research teams.

The position is based in Redmond, Washington, and pays between $35 and $55 per hour on a 12-month contract. Applicants need a relevant bachelor’s degree or equivalent experience, alongside at least two years working in areas such as digital archiving or records management.

However, while the move could help safeguard Nintendo’s history, it doesn’t necessarily mean that more classic games will become available to players.

Nintendo fan’s $100K game collection stolen after spending 40 years building it

The role focuses on preserving internal information rather than restoring old titles or releasing them on modern platforms.

#10920. Redmagic 12 Pro review: You’ve never gamed on a phone like this before

The Redmagic 12 Pro impresses with a Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 processor, liquid cooling, a fan, plus a massive 8,400mAh battery to keep the games going for hours and hours.

As I sat in my living room with my family, playing Monster Hunter Outlanders together on the Redmagic 12 Pro, I completely forgot I was playing on a phone. The game looked perfect. It ran at a locked 60FPS and hadn’t dropped frames since I began playing over an hour ago. It just felt like I was back in college, playing Monster Hunter Freedom on my PSP with my friends in the same room.

Most other phones at this point would have throttled hard. What began at 60FPS almost certainly would have been 40FPS tops after 30 minutes or so; the frame drops during heavy fights would have been even worse, but the Redmagic 12 Pro didn’t skip a beat. Even after a solid hour of playing, the battery had only dropped 10%, completely erasing all concerns about using my phone as a gaming console.

This thing is the real deal, and while I’ve used plenty of high-performance phones and gaming phones over the years, none have quite nailed it as this one has. Now, with my Redmagic 12 Pro and my trusty GameSir G8 Galileo, I can game whenever and wherever I want without battery anxiety or thermal problems. Redmagic has gotten really good at this gaming thing.

Sustained gaming performance is difficult to pull off on a phone, which is why the Redmagic 12 Pro features two types of active cooling: a fan and liquid cooling. This design builds on last year’s with a larger liquid cooling system and a more efficient fan. The intake and exhaust vents are on the sides of the phone, not the back, ensuring these shouldn’t get easily blocked. Despite this, the phone is IP68 and IP69 rated.

Most modern phones have huge camera islands that make them awkward to use in controllers, but the Redmagic 12 Pro features a completely flat build that fits perfectly in *any* controller. I don’t usually like overly flat builds, but it actually suits this phone’s purpose.

This should mean the phone does exceedingly well in benchmarks, but I have the distinct feeling the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 processor inside is making that overly difficult. I don’t have any other SD8 Elite Gen 6 phones just yet, but rumor has it that this is one *hot* chipset. I saw the phone hit 50C during testing, so that definitely seems to be the case.

In 3DMark Solar Bay Extreme, a benchmark that stresses ray-tracing performance over 20 minutes, the Redmagic 12 Pro started at 2065 points and ended at 1169 on its last run. That’s a 43% drop in performance over a 20-minute period, which is in line with passively-cooled phones like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Regardless, the initial performance is nearly double the S26 Ultra, while the 1169 end score matches the S26 Ultra’s best score and is roughly 35% higher than the S26 Ultra’s sustained performance score of around 860.

Actual gaming seemed to fare better in my testing, where I saw sustained performance consistently outperform any Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 phone I have in my possession. When running Wanderstop through the phone’s built-in Steam emulation (more on that soon), the Redmagic 12 Pro averaged 72FPS at the highest settings. Other phones with SD8 Elite Gen 5 processors scored half that, at best. Native Android games like Monster Hunter Outlanders worked even better.

But it’s not just performance that sets this phone apart; it’s the gaming features. Redmagic has fully leaned into the mobile gaming power user angle with its software. Pressing and holding the red button on the right side of the phone launches Gravity X, the built-in game launcher that keeps all your games in one place. It even features an embedded Steam emulator, so all you need to do is sign in with your Steam account and download anything in your library.

Overall performance on Steam games is impressive, but like any emulation, not everything is going to work. Hogwarts Legacy worked near-flawlessly at medium settings, but titles like Star Wars Galactic Racer wouldn’t run at all. Other apps that run Steam games, like DroidDeck and Game Native, also work, but the Adreno 850 GPU doesn’t have official support on those apps yet, so compatibility is very hit or miss (mostly miss) for now.

The Redmagic 12 Pro’s in-game menu is also unlike anything you’ve ever seen. It’s not just jam-packed with information, but has a boatload of bespoke features. One custom mapping tool lets you map the triggers (yes, this phone has capacitive triggers on the side) to any on-screen virtual key.

Another utility records all the time, Xbox/PS5/Switch style, and saves a clip at the touch of a floating button. Yet another features on on-screen AI anime character that can interact with your games and even give you gameplay feedback. You can even enable frame gen and Super Resolution in any game. It’s wild.

All this is wrapped up in an incredibly impressive hardware package, with loud, clear speakers, an excellent 185Hz AMOLED display (that uses DC dimming and 2160Hz PWM for eye comfort), with 5,000-nit peak brightness. I measured the actual HDR brightness at 2300-nits, so this can get nice and bright when it needs to.

If there was ever a phone that could play games and still get you through a full day’s normal phone usage, it’s this one. Passthrough charging is also supported, too, so you can keep the battery from getting overcharged or overheated while gaming for hours.

As is the case with many laser-focused devices like this, the camera is the low point of the experience. It’s got a 50MP rear camera and a 50MP ultra wide. Both get the job done, but they’ll never impress you.

The 16MP 9th-generation under display camera looks impressive on the surface — you can’t see it at all, even with light shining off the display — but the results are still far from good. Redmagic uses an AI algorithm to substantially enhance the overall quality, which cleans up the image nicely but exhibits lots of hallucinations. Just look at my eyes in the above picture. It’s not good.

Thankfully, the Android 17-based Redmagic OS 12 software is quite good. Visually, it reminds me of a mix of AOSP and Samsung’s One UI. Lots of expected features are here — an AI wallpaper generator, call screening, recording with transcripts, writing help, etc — plus all the awesome gaming-focused tools and quite a bit of customization across the OS.

It’s a great overall experience, but Redmagic only offers software updates for 3 years, so this one won’t stay around as long as some other phones. If you’re more interested in mobile gaming than anything — especially if you want a phone that can easily run Steam games in addition to mobile stuff — this is an impressively good choice.

#10919. Amazon customers have a few weeks to claim share of $309 million settlement

Amazon customers who experienced problems receiving refunds for returned products could be eligible for compensation from a $309.5 million class action settlement, but the deadline to submit a claim is approaching.

The settlement follows a lawsuit accusing Amazon of improperly handling customer returns, including failing to issue refunds, providing incorrect refunds, and charging customers again after they had returned items.

Amazon has denied any wrongdoing but agreed to a $309.5 million settlement, which impacted customers can claim their share of.

The company also began issuing refunds in 2025 after identifying transactions where payments had not been completed or it could not verify that the correct items had been returned.

According to court documents, Amazon has already issued approximately $570 million in refunds, with additional payments expected as part of the agreement.

Customers may qualify if they purchased a physical product through Amazon.com, received it in the United States, and initiated a return or requested a refund between September 5, 2017, and February 12, 2026.

To be eligible, customers must have experienced at least one of the following:

The settlement divides eligible customers into two groups, with different requirements for receiving compensation.

Customers in Subclass A, whose qualifying transactions have already been identified through Amazon’s records, will automatically receive payments and do not need to submit a claim.

Those in Subclass B must submit a valid claim before December 1, 2026, to receive compensation. Customers can check their eligibility and file a claim through the official settlement website.

AT&T customers could soon receive up to $5,000 from $177 million data breach settlement

Amazon Prime users have days left to claim share of $2.5bn settlement

Millions of iPhone owners can now claim up to $95 from Apple Siri AI settlement

The amount customers receive will depend on their individual refund issues and the settlement’s distribution process, so there is no fixed payout.

The December 1 deadline also applies to customers wishing to exclude themselves from the settlement or submit an objection.

However, payments are not guaranteed yet. A final approval hearing is scheduled for March 16, 2027, and compensation will only be distributed once the court approves the agreement and it becomes final.

Amazon has maintained that it did not violate consumer protection laws, and the court has made no finding of wrongdoing.

#10917. How to Quickly Check What Your HTML Code Looks Like in a Browser

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Writing HTML is one of the main parts of building a website. It is mainly responsible for webpage structure and content.

A small error can result in layout problems. Oftentimes, you don't notice these issues until you preview the page in a browser. A quick way to ensure that your layout looks the same as you want is to preview your HTML code.

In this blog, we’ll show you easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time. So keep on reading till the end.

The following are two easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time.

This is a simple way to preview your HTML. You don't need a web server to preview your HTML code. You have to follow the following steps.

Open a code editor or simply Notepad on your device. Create a new file and paste your HTML inside it.

Step 2 - Save the File With an HTML Extension

After adding your HTML code in Notepad, save the file as index.html. You have to ensure that the extension is .html, not .txt.

If you are using Notepad on Windows, click on “File” and navigate to the “Save As” option and click it. Then enter index.html as the filename. Make sure that Notepad doesn’t save it as a text document.

Locate the saved file on your device. Double-click to open it in your default browser. The browser will render the HTML elements to show the headings, paragraphs, and buttons on the page, as you can see in the image below.

This is how you can create a simple file to preview your HTML before deploying it. If you see any errors, you can correct them in your file and preview it again.

However, it is worth considering that HTML may not show the complete website behavior alone. The final result can also depend on JavaScript, external CSS, or other resources. You should check those files as well.

This method provides an easy way to preview your HTML code. It involves using a specialized online HTML viewer. This approach is even quicker and doesn't even require you to create a local file first.

Step 1 - Open the HTML Viewer Open your web browser and search for an HTML viewer. You may see multiple tools in the search results. Select one that allows HTML preview online. For demonstration purposes, we are using HTML Viewer by minifier.org.

Once you’ve selected the tool, open it. Navigate to its input box and paste your HTML code into it. We are using the same HTML code from the first example.

You can also upload your file or paste its URL into the tool. After entering your HTML code, click on the “View” button to preview your HTML code.

In the screenshot above, you can see the tool showing the HTML preview on the right side. This tool also gives you the option to edit your HTML code and see the results in real time through the “Auto-update” option.

If the auto-update option is checked, you can see the changes in real time as you edit the code. This makes editing much easier, as you can modify the code and preview the changes immediately without returning to your original file. It also allows you to verify how your layout will appear on desktop, tablet, or mobile. You can check this by clicking on the icons provided in the top right corner of the tool. Once you are satisfied with the output, you can copy or download your edited HTML file easily.

This tool also allows you to test the HTML code of your game or video in real time, as you can see in the screenshot below.

These features make such specialized HTML viewers practical. These are quick and easy to use. However, the features shown above may not be available on all the tools. If you are specifically looking for such features, you can read the tool’s description for these features before using it.

HTML is one of the main components of a website. It defines the structure and content of webpages. Many users need to check how their HTML code will look in a browser to identify potential issues before deployment.

To preview your HTML code, you can use simple methods. One is to paste your code into Notepad and save it as index.html. Then open it in your browser to get a preview of your HTML code.

You can also use a specialized HTML viewer. Such tools can work by just pasting the code into the input box. Once you paste your code, click on the view button to get a preview of your HTML code. Some tools also offer various other features, such as editing, viewing games, and videos in real time.

Both of these methods work fine. The choice depends on the user's preferences and needs.

In the November 2026 Google System Update for Android, Google confirms that it is about to roll out a new “Family Hub” feature in Settings. This is a central location for family account settings, as Google explains:

You can now access the Android Family Hub, a dedicated space for family management tools in Android Settings.

Google could start rolling out this feature at any time now, but we’ve yet to see any evidence that it is widely showing up for users at this time.

#10908. AI Testing Tools: Benefits, Limitations, and Best Practices

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every vendor says the same thing: buy our AI, and testing gets easy. Don't believe it. AI testing tools do help, though. Some teams save real hours with them, while others waste a quarter and a budget. The difference usually comes down to how they start.

They support your QA team by writing test cases, running them, spotting failures and suggesting repairs. Some tools read a requirements document. Others watch real users click around and then replay what they did. Playwright, for instance, now ships built-in test agents: a planner that explores your app and writes a test plan, a generator that turns the plan into test files, and a healer that runs the suite and tries to repair failing tests.

Old-school automation needs a person to write every script. Then a developer moves one button and the whole thing falls over. Somebody loses a morning to repairs, and QA engineers hate that part of the job.

Quality is the big worry. Generated tests can look clever and prove nothing. A tool might confirm that a checkout page opens and then mark checkout as covered, but opening a page is not testing it. Even Playwright's documentation says generated tests may contain initial errors and that you should review what the healer changes before accepting it. A person has to read the output every time.

Business logic is the next problem. The tool has no idea that your refund window dropped from 30 days to 14, and it cannot feel that a signup form is confusing. Intent lives in people's heads.

Coverage numbers can mislead. A dashboard says 90%, and the room relaxes. Meanwhile, the odd edge cases nobody thought of sit untouched. Bugs do not read dashboards.

Check fit first. Does it cover web, mobile and API tests, and does it work with your stack? Ask before any sales call, not after. Then test its honesty: when something fails, a good tool tells you why. A vague red mark costs your team time because nobody can verify it. Read the pricing page slowly, too, since some vendors charge per seat and some per test run.

Finally, throw your ugliest feature at it. Demos use tidy little apps, but your product has pop-ups, slow pages and odd corners. Watch how the tool handles those.

Expecting instant results. People switch the tool on and wait for magic. What they get is noisy reports and grumpy engineers. Some managers even cut QA headcount right away, which is a bad idea: somebody has to tune the tool and handle the cases it cannot. Good testers become more valuable, not less.

Skipping training. Your team needs to learn how the tool behaves. Two short workshops and a shared doc go a long way.

Trusting machine-written text without a second look. AI tools now draft test descriptions, bug reports and release notes too. If your team wants to know how much of a document was machine-generated, an AI-content checker such as ZeroGPT can give a rough signal. Treat the result as a hint rather than proof, because such detectors make mistakes and publish their own accuracy claims.

Agents are the obvious next step. Instead of running fixed scripts, they poke around your app like a curious user, clicking, typing and reporting anything strange. Early versions already exist, and they are rough but improving.

Editors will change as well. Your code editor may soon suggest a test the moment you finish a function, and some bug reports may arrive with a fix attached. The wall between development and QA is getting thin.

Yes, but carefully. AI testing tools will not replace your QA team. They take the boring work off testers' hands and spot patterns people might miss, but they still need a human to say "that result is wrong." Start small, measure what happens and keep your testers close to the tool. Do that, and you will ship faster without losing control.

#10902. Microsoft Authenticator is ditching its own password backup for Google One

Microsoft is ditching its own password backup option for the Microsoft Authenticator app for Android and instead using Google One for that service.

Microsoft Authenticator is a one-time password / two-factor authentication app that works more or less like Google Authenticator. The app on your phone spits out a one-time code that you’ll use for logging into various online accounts.

Starting in January, though, Microsoft Authenticator is doing away with Microsoft Account backup for your one-time password connections. Microsoft detailed the change on a support page, explaining that backup on Android will now run through Google One backup. Users will need to toggle on Microsoft Authenticator in Google One’s backup settings on their device.

Starting January 2027, you will no longer use your Microsoft personal account to enable backup. You will need to enable “Microsoft Authenticator” from Google One backup.

It’s an interesting change, to say the least, but it’s not unprecedented. Microsoft Authenticator already relied on iCloud Backup on iOS to back up one-time passwords and accounts.

The change kicks in starting in January of next year, which is mere weeks away at this point.

#10897. Testing JSON-LD Like an API: 7 Checks Before Your Next Release

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A content editor changes an article’s headline. The page updates immediately, but its JSON-LD still contains the previous title. A redesign introduces a new author component while an old plugin continues publishing a different author name in the page source.

Neither failure necessarily produces a JavaScript error. Both leave machines and readers with conflicting descriptions of the same page.

For developers, the useful approach is to treat JSON-LD as a maintained output of the application, with a data source, validation rules, and regression tests. This guide uses a blog template to show how. You will need basic JavaScript knowledge and a modern Node.js installation for the runnable tests.

Requests for search improvements often arrive as broad outcomes: improve discoverability, clarify entities, or support answer engines. These need to become implementation requirements before development starts.

For example, AEO directory includes structured data within descriptions of broader optimization services. For an engineering team, the actionable deliverable is a page representation whose types, properties, and values can be checked against the published content. I personally used this , and adding this because this can help other developer's and designer for optimization and get higher search result's.

For a blog template, define these acceptance criteria:

Keep implementation success separate from search outcomes. Google’s AI features guidance says there is no special Schema.org markup required for its AI search features. Correct markup also does not guarantee rich results or inclusion in an AI-generated answer.

Avoid separate, manually maintained copies of the headline, author, and publication date. Both the visible template and metadata builder should consume the same published record.

Save the following as article-schema.mjs. The builder assumes an upstream content-validation step has already checked required strings, timestamps, and approved HTTPS URLs.

This is a deliberately small example, not a complete rich-result eligibility checklist. Add relevant images and other supported properties when the real page supplies them. Consult the Schema.org BlogPosting definition and Google’s Article documentation for their respective vocabularies and search guidance.

Use a consistent canonical-URL policy before building the identifier: normalize the intended hostname, trailing-slash convention, and query parameters. Do not generate a fresh UUID for the same article on every request.

SitePoint’s guide to implementing JSON-LD on modern websites covers embedding approaches across common stacks.

Valid JSON is not automatically safe to insert into a raw HTML script element.

A CMS value containing can terminate the element at the HTML-parser level, even when the value belongs to a JSON string. Concatenating a headline directly into a script tag is particularly risky.

Append this helper to article-schema.mjs:

JSON serialization handles quotes and backslashes. Replacing literal less-than characters prevents an embedded closing-script sequence from appearing in the resulting text. The Next.js JSON-LD guide documents this approach for script embedding.

Use the serialized result as the content of a script element with type="application/ld+json". Follow your framework’s documented embedding mechanism so the JSON is neither double-encoded nor inserted through an unrelated HTML context.

This helper is specific to JSON-LD script content. It is not a general-purpose HTML sanitizer, URL validator, or substitute for validating CMS data.

The tests should catch changes that would make metadata misleading or unsafe, not merely confirm that an object exists.

Save this file as article-schema.test.mjs beside the builder:

These are unit tests for the builder and serializer. They do not prove that a template embeds the script correctly or that the deployed page is eligible for a search feature. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing helps distinguish those responsibilities.

A passing builder test cannot detect a page component reading a different CMS field.

Add a browser-level check against a controlled article fixture. Inspect its visible heading, byline, dates, canonical link, and JSON-LD together. For this template, assert that the intended article entity appears once and matches those visible fields.

Do not assert that the entire page contains exactly one JSON-LD script. A site may legitimately publish organization, breadcrumb, and article entities separately. Instead, identify the relevant entity by its @id and detect conflicting definitions.

Include these failure cases in the test plan:

Prefer generating metadata from the content loaded during server rendering or static generation when that fits the application. If it is injected client-side, inspect the rendered result and test navigation explicitly. Avoid making essential metadata depend on a user interaction.

No single tool establishes syntax, semantic accuracy, search eligibility, and content consistency at once.

Test both representative code samples and deployed URLs. A validator cannot confirm the truth of an author name simply because the property is syntactically valid.

Google’s general structured-data guidelines also require markup to represent the page accurately. Treat validation warnings as findings to investigate; do not add invented reviews, dates, or attributes merely to improve a tool’s output.

Ownership matters after the first release. Decide which application layer generates article markup, who maintains its mapping, and which publishing events invalidate cached output.

When an editor changes a headline, the visible page and JSON-LD should be rebuilt or refreshed together. Updating one while serving the other from an older cache creates a mismatch even if both implementations are individually correct.

Avoid setting dateModified to the current time on every deployment. A CSS update or infrastructure rebuild does not necessarily mean the article changed. Read the timestamp from the editorial record instead.

Before shipping a template change, run the builder tests, inspect one rendered page per affected template, and check representative deployed URLs. Keep a small fixture set that includes optional fields, long text, translated content, and script-like strings.

A reliable JSON-LD implementation has a traceable relationship with published content. When someone changes the page, its machine-readable description should change predictably—and the test suite should expose the cases where it does not.

#10891. A tiny light trap could unlock million qubit quantum computers

After years of slow progress, researchers may finally be seeing a clear path forward in the quest to build powerful quantum computers. These machines are expected to dramatically shorten the time required for certain calculations, turning problems that would take classical computers thousands of years into tasks that could be completed in hours.

A team led by physicists at Stanford University has developed a new kind of optical cavity that can efficiently capture single photons, the basic particles of light, emitted by individual atoms. Those atoms serve as the core components of a quantum computer because they store qubits, which are the quantum equivalent of the zeros and ones used in traditional computing. For the first time, this approach allows information to be collected from all qubits at once.

Optical Cavities Enable Faster Qubit Readout

In research published in Nature, the team describes a system made up of 40 optical cavities, each holding a single atom qubit, along with a larger prototype that contains more than 500 cavities. The results point to a realistic route toward building quantum computing networks that could one day include as many as a million qubits.

"If we want to make a quantum computer, we need to be able to read information out of the quantum bits very quickly," said Jon Simon, the study's senior author and associate professor of physics and of applied physics in Stanford's School of Humanities and Sciences. "Until now, there hasn't been a practical way to do that at scale because atoms just don't emit light fast enough, and on top of that, they spew it out in all directions. An optical cavity can efficiently guide emitted light toward a particular direction, and now we've found a way to equip each atom in a quantum computer within its own individual cavity."

An optical cavity works by trapping light between two or more reflective surfaces, causing it to bounce back and forth. The effect can be compared to standing between mirrors in a fun house, where reflections seem to stretch endlessly into the distance. In scientific settings, these cavities are far smaller and use repeated passes of a laser beam to extract information from atoms.

Although optical cavities have been studied for decades, they have been difficult to use with atoms because atoms are extremely small and nearly transparent. Getting light to interact with them strongly enough has been a persistent challenge.

Rather than relying on many repeated reflections, the Stanford team introduced microlenses inside each cavity to tightly focus light onto a single atom. Even with fewer light bounces, this method proved more effective at pulling quantum information from the atom.

"We have developed a new type of cavity architecture; it's not just two mirrors anymore," said Adam Shaw, a Stanford Science Fellow and first author on the study. "We hope this will enable us to build dramatically faster, distributed quantum computers that can talk to each other with much faster data rates."

Beyond the Binary Limits of Classical Computing

Conventional computers process information using bits that represent either zero or one. Quantum computers operate using qubits, which are based on the quantum states of tiny particles. A qubit can represent zero, one, or both states at the same time, allowing quantum systems to handle certain calculations far more efficiently than classical machines.

"A classical computer has to churn through possibilities one by one, looking for the correct answer," said Simon. "But a quantum computer acts like noise-canceling headphones that compare combinations of answers, amplifying the right ones while muffling the wrong ones."

Scientists estimate that quantum computers will need millions of qubits to outperform today's most powerful supercomputers. According to Simon, reaching that level will likely require connecting many quantum computers into large networks. The parallel light-based interface demonstrated in this study provides an efficient foundation for scaling up to those sizes.

The researchers showed a working 40-cavity array in the current study, along with a proof-of-concept system containing more than 500 cavities. Their next goal is to expand to tens of thousands. Looking further ahead, the team envisions quantum data centers in which individual quantum computers are linked through cavity-based network interfaces to form full-scale quantum supercomputers.

Broader Scientific and Technological Impact

Significant engineering hurdles remain, but the researchers believe the potential benefits are substantial. Large-scale quantum computers could lead to breakthroughs in materials design and chemical synthesis, including applications related to drug discovery, as well as advances in code breaking.

The ability to efficiently collect light also has implications beyond computing. Cavity arrays could improve biosensing and microscopy, supporting progress in medical and biological research. Quantum networks may even contribute to astronomy by enabling optical telescopes with enhanced resolution, potentially allowing scientists to directly observe planets orbiting stars beyond our solar system.

"As we understand more about how to manipulate light at a single particle level, I think it will transform our ability to see the world," Shaw said.

​​Simon is also the Joan Reinhart Professor of Physics & Applied Physics. Shaw is also a Felix Bloch Fellow and an Urbanek-Chodorow Fellow.

Additional Stanford co-authors include David Schuster, the Joan Reinhart Professor of Applied Physics, and doctoral students Anna Soper, Danial Shadmany, and Da-Yeon Koh.

Other co-authors include researchers from Stony Brook University, the University of Chicago, Harvard University, and Montana State University.

This research received support from the National Science Foundation, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, Army Research Office, Hertz Foundation, and the U.S. Department of Defense.

Matt Jaffe of Montana State University and Simon act as consultants to and hold stock options in Atom Computing. Shadmany, Jaffe, Schuster, and Simon, as well as Aishwarya Kumar of Stony Brook, hold a patent on the resonator geometry demonstrated in this work.

Materials provided by Stanford University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10890. ‘Cyclon’ uses an old Google Pixel to let AI do literally everything for you – who wants this?

As the AI era continues to find its footing in the consumer market, “Cyclon” is a new smartphone/software experience that, to start, is reusing old Google Pixel phones with a software experience that literally uses AI to do every little thing. And my biggest question is just… who actually wants this?

The pitch of Cyclon, the founders explain in a video on Twitter/X, is to “[build] an AI phone to replace the iPhone.” And the central experience seems to be… talking to a chatbot. Examples in the video include telling the AI to “handle my notifications” and letting it monitor a WhatsApp message and reply to a friend asking for photos by grabbing those photos from your gallery without you as the user needing to touch the device.

Other examples on the company’s website have mixed utility at a glance. “Order my usual on DoorDash” and calling a courier to let them into a building certainly could be useful, but “tell the group chat I’m running ten minutes late” only to have the AI send the message on WhatsApp “in your words” not only seems like a waste of time, but a pointless disconnect from reality (as is all too common with these AI ventures).

The one thing that caught my attention here is building apps on demand and being able to share them. The example given is a language learning app tailored to a specific vacation. It’s a really cool idea, though one I would imagine will have some rocky edges. Google is on a similar track with Gemini Intelligence’s “Create my widget” feature (on that note, when is that releasing on phones Google?), and I’d imagine Gemini will be able to do the same thing in time.

The actual hardware side of things is also… interesting.

Cyclon says that it has a working prototype of the idea running on Google Pixel 8a units, and it’ll ship “first-batch” backers one of those units with their $199 pre-order of a later “Cyclon Phone” that itself is expected to cost $699 – so you’re getting two phones for $699. That alone seems a little questionable in the midst of RAMageddon, but I digress.

That “final” phone hardware… sure is something.

There are some good ideas here, some big promises, but more than anything I just want to know who actually wants to use a phone like this.

I’m all for the idea of AI making my life a little easier (despite finding hardly any ways it can do that so far), but sending everything through a chatbot that does everything in the background and even generates “your words” for responses to my friends and family just sounds awful. And the state of social media isn’t exactly helping matters here. Twitter/X is 90% AI replies for engagement for this sort of thing, and Cyclon is paying influencers to boost that engagement too, so genuine reactions just seem impossible to find.

Sound off in the comments below if you’re an actual human with an opinion on this one, because I’m dying to know what real people think of this.

#10887. Scientists create smart synthetic skin that can hide images and change shape

Synthetic materials are widely used across science, engineering, and industry, but most are designed to perform only a narrow range of tasks. A research team at Penn State set out to change that. Led by Hongtao Sun, assistant professor of industrial and manufacturing engineering (IME), the group developed a new fabrication technique that can produce multifunctional "smart synthetic skin." These adaptable materials can be programmed to perform a wide variety of tasks, including hiding or revealing information, enabling adaptive camouflage, and supporting soft robotic systems.

Using this new approach, the researchers created a programmable smart skin made from hydrogel, a soft, water-rich material. Unlike conventional synthetic materials with fixed behaviors, this smart skin can be tuned to respond in multiple ways. Its appearance, mechanical behavior, surface texture, and ability to change shape can all be adjusted when the material is exposed to external triggers such as heat, solvents, or physical stress.

The findings were published in Nature Communications, where the study was also selected for Editors' Highlights.

Inspired by Octopus Skin and Living Systems

Sun, the project's principal investigator, said the concept was inspired by cephalopods such as octopuses, which can rapidly alter the look and texture of their skin. These animals use such changes to blend into their surroundings or communicate with one another.

"Cephalopods use a complex system of muscles and nerves to exhibit dynamic control over the appearance and texture of their skin," Sun said. "Inspired by these soft organisms, we developed a 4D-printing system to capture that idea in a synthetic, soft material."

Sun also holds affiliations in biomedical engineering, material science and engineering, and the Materials Research Institute at Penn State. He described the process as 4D printing because the printed objects are not static. Instead, they can actively change in response to environmental conditions.

Printing Digital Instructions Into Material

To achieve this adaptability, the team used a method called halftone-encoded printing. This technique converts image or texture data into binary ones and zeros and embeds that information directly into the material. The approach is similar to how dot patterns are used in newspapers or photographs to create images.

By encoding these digital patterns within the hydrogel, the researchers can program how the smart skin reacts to different stimuli. The printed patterns determine how various regions of the material respond. Some areas may swell, shrink, or soften more than others when exposed to temperature changes, liquids, or mechanical forces. By carefully designing these patterns, the team can control the material's overall behavior.

"In simple terms, we're printing instructions into the material," Sun explained. "Those instructions tell the skin how to react when something changes around it."

One of the most eye-catching demonstrations involved the material's ability to conceal and reveal visual information. Haoqing Yang, a doctoral candidate in IME and the paper's first author, said this capability highlights the potential of the smart skin.

To demonstrate the effect, the team encoded an image of the Mona Lisa into the hydrogel film. When the material was washed with ethanol, it appeared transparent and showed no visible image. The hidden image became clear only after the film was placed in ice water or gradually heated.

Yang noted that the Mona Lisa was used only as an example. The printing technique allows virtually any image to be encoded into the hydrogel.

"This behavior could be used for camouflage, where a surface blends into its environment, or for information encryption, where messages are hidden and only revealed under specific conditions," Yang said.

The researchers also showed that concealed patterns could be detected by gently stretching the material and analyzing how it deforms using digital image correlation analysis. This means information can be revealed not only visually, but also through mechanical interaction, adding an extra level of security.

The smart skin also demonstrated remarkable flexibility. According to Sun, the material can easily shift from a flat sheet into complex, bio-inspired shapes with detailed surface textures. Unlike many other shape-changing materials, this transformation does not require multiple layers or different substances.

Instead, the changes in shape and texture are controlled entirely by the digitally printed halftone patterns within a single sheet. This allows the material to replicate effects similar to those seen in cephalopod skin.

Building on this capability, the team showed that multiple functions can be programmed to work together. By carefully designing the halftone patterns, they encoded the Mona Lisa image into flat films that later transformed into three-dimensional forms. As the sheets curved into dome-like shapes, the hidden image slowly appeared, showing that changes in shape and visual appearance can be coordinated within one material.

"Similar to how cephalopods coordinate body shape and skin patterning, the synthetic smart skin can simultaneously control what it looks like and how it deforms, all within a single, soft material," Sun said.

Expanding the Potential of 4D-Printed Hydrogels

Sun said the new work builds on earlier research by the team on 4D-printed smart hydrogels, which was also published in Nature Communications. That earlier study focused on combining mechanical properties with programmable transitions from flat to three-dimensional forms. In the current research, the team expanded the approach by using halftone-encoded 4D printing to integrate even more functions into a single hydrogel film.

Looking ahead, the researchers aim to create a scalable and versatile platform that allows precise digital encoding of multiple functions within one adaptive material.

"This interdisciplinary research at the intersection of advanced manufacturing, intelligent materials and mechanics opens new opportunities with broad implications for stimulus-responsive systems, biomimetic engineering, advanced encryption technologies, biomedical devices and more," Sun said.

The study also included Penn State co-authors Haotian Li and Juchen Zhang, both doctoral candidates in IME, and Tengxiao Liu, a lecturer in biomedical engineering. H. Jerry Qi, professor of mechanical engineering at Georgia Institute of Technology, also collaborated on the project.

Materials provided by Penn State. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10885. How to Create an App in ChatGPT in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning how to create an app in ChatGPT starts with the Jotform App Builder plugin (listed in ChatGPT's plugin directory as Jotform Apps): install it, connect a Jotform account, describe the app, approve the requested action, and refine the result. The plugin creates a Jotform App containing pages, forms, and resources that can be edited in Jotform App Builder and shared with its intended audience.

ChatGPT helps turn an idea into a clear request and supports revisions through conversation. The Jotform App Builder plugin performs the app-building actions, while the App Builder in Jotform provides the editor for closer customization.

This no-code ChatGPT app builder workflow suits projects built around information, forms, and everyday tasks.

"ChatGPT app development" can also describe coding projects. OpenAI's documentation on building plugins for ChatGPT describes a package of skills, an optional MCP server that connects to tools and data through the Model Context Protocol, optional UI, authentication, and publishing. The two routes differ like this:

This article covers the no-code route. The result is a Jotform App; the workflow does not include writing and deploying a separate codebase.

A Jotform App can organize pages, forms, and resources for customers, employees, members, or event attendees. The most useful first project has one audience and one clear purpose. Here are some examples:

These are starting structures. A booking form, for example, still needs its availability settings checked. An ordering app needs its order process reviewed, and any payment setup requires separate attention.

Setup starts with installing the plugin in ChatGPT and authorizing access to the intended Jotform account. Jotform's own how-to guide lists the steps.

To create an app in ChatGPT, select the installed plugin in a new chat and enter a specific description. Include the audience, purpose, pages, and design direction.

This gives the AI app builder a defined structure. The studio should then supply its real booking URL, approved resource links, contact details, and onboarding copy during review. It is important to review every generated form, link, and business rule before use.

A good prompt explains who will use the app, what they need to accomplish, and which elements support that task. Visual preferences help, but purpose and content should come first.

"Build an app for my business" leaves the audience, structure, and actions unclear. The design-studio example identifies a client audience, an onboarding task, specific pages, and a color scheme. Before submitting a request, define:

Request the essentials to build a Jotform App around one process, then expand.

The plugin supports follow-up requests in the same chat. Smaller changes make it easier to review each update and identify anything that needs correction. For the studio onboarding app, some follow-ups could include:

Each request should be followed by a review of the result. A map needs the correct address; a gallery needs suitable images; a booking form needs appropriate fields and configuration.

Select Edit in Jotform when you need closer editing. In Jotform App Builder, inspect every page, element, form, link, label, and visual choice. Replace the sample content and check that navigation follows the client's expected path. Third-party integrations require manual setup in the builder, so requesting a booking feature should not be treated as confirmation that an external calendar has been connected.

Select Share App in the generated result to open the available sharing options. For detailed distribution and access settings, open the app in Jotform App Builder and use the Publish tab and Quick Share. Jotform's sharing guide covers each option.

The builder supports copying the app link, inviting people by email, social sharing, and downloading a QR code. Choose the method that fits your audience: a client can receive a link, while event attendees may find a QR code more useful.

Review access before distributing the app, because newly created apps are public by default. Check exactly what a visitor can open before publishing.

Test the Jotform App as an intended user would, using fictional information first. Review it on both desktop and mobile before collecting real submissions.

Creating an app with the plugin is free to start, and Jotform offers a free Starter plan, but app usage remains subject to your account's allowances. A Jotform support reply from 2024 describes Starter as up to five forms and 100 monthly submissions. These are account allowances, rather than a separate allocation for each generated app, and they may have changed, so confirm them on Jotform's current pricing page. Paid plans provide more submissions.

Before you launch, compare expected usage with the current account limits. An onboarding app serving a few clients has different requirements from an event app collecting hundreds of registrations.

The most common mistakes are unclear requests, oversized first versions, and sharing before testing. Each can be reduced by defining a smaller task and reviewing the result.

This workflow is best for defined business tasks. Projects requiring extensive custom backend behavior need a separate assessment, such as the developer route described above.

Before sharing, confirm that the app serves its intended audience and that its content, behavior, and settings have been reviewed. A short launch checklist helps keep the review consistent.

Here are some frequently asked questions about the output, account requirements, and practical limits of the Jotform App Builder plugin.

Yes. With the plugin installed and authorized, plain-language requests can generate and revise an app in ChatGPT.

The plugin can create a Jotform App combining pages, forms, and resources. Follow-up prompts can refine its structure, content, and design.

Customer portals, internal tools, event apps, business websites, directories, onboarding hubs, and booking or ordering apps are some of the suitable starting points.

A Jotform App is the finished app created and maintained through Jotform. A custom GPT configures a conversational assistant, while a plugin or application made with OpenAI developer tools involves a separate development workflow with its own build, authentication, and publishing steps.

Understanding how to create an app in ChatGPT means keeping the roles clear: ChatGPT supports the conversation, the plugin creates and revises the Jotform App, and the Jotform editor handles closer editing and configuration.

Start with a focused task, then review the content, permissions, and behavior. Test the complete experience with sample information before sharing it with the intended audience.

#10883. AI reads brain MRIs in seconds and flags emergencies

A newly developed artificial intelligence system from the University of Michigan can analyze brain MRI scans and deliver a diagnosis in a matter of seconds, according to a new study. The model identified neurological conditions with accuracy reaching 97.5% and was also able to assess how urgently patients needed medical care.

Researchers say this first-of-its-kind technology has the potential to reshape how brain imaging is handled across health systems in the United States. The findings were published in Nature Biomedical Engineering.

"As the global demand for MRI rises and places significant strain our physicians and health systems, our AI model has potential to reduce burden by improving diagnosis and treatment with fast, accurate information," said senior author Todd Hollon, M.D., a neurosurgeon at University of Michigan Health and assistant professor of neurosurgery at U-M Medical School.

Hollon named the new technology Prima. Over a one-year period, his research team evaluated the system using more than 30,000 MRI studies.

Across more than 50 different radiologic diagnoses involving major neurological disorders, Prima delivered stronger diagnostic performance than other advanced AI models. In addition to identifying disease, the system also proved capable of determining which cases required higher priority.

Certain neurological conditions, including strokes and brain hemorrhages, demand immediate medical attention. Hollon said that in these situations, Prima can automatically alert health care providers so action can be taken quickly.

The system was designed to notify the most appropriate subspecialist, such as a stroke neurologist or neurosurgeon. Feedback becomes available immediately after a patient completes imaging.

"Accuracy is paramount when reading a brain MRI, but quick turnaround times are critical for timely diagnosis and improved outcomes," said Yiwei Lyu, M.S., co-first author and postdoctoral fellow of Computer Science and Engineering at U-M.

"At key steps in the process, our results show how Prima can improve workflows and streamline clinical care without abandoning accuracy."

Prima is classified as a vision language model (VLM), a type of artificial intelligence that can process images, video, and text together in real time. While artificial intelligence has been applied to MRI analysis before, researchers say Prima takes a different approach.

Earlier models were typically trained on carefully selected subsets of MRI data and designed to perform narrow tasks, such as identifying lesions or estimating dementia risk. Prima was trained on a much broader dataset.

Hollon's team used every available MRI collected since radiology records were digitized at University of Michigan Health. This included more than 200,000 MRI studies and 5.6 million imaging sequences. The model also incorporated patients' clinical histories and the reasons physicians ordered each imaging study.

"Prima works like a radiologist by integrating information regarding the patient's medical history and imaging data to produce a comprehensive understanding of their health," said co-first author Samir Harake, a data scientist in Hollon's Machine Learning in Neurosurgery Lab.

"This enables better performance across a broad range of prediction tasks."

Addressing MRI Delays and Radiology Shortages

Each year, millions of MRI scans are performed worldwide, many of them focused on neurological disease. Researchers say the demand for these scans is growing faster than the availability of neuroradiology services.

This imbalance has contributed to staffing shortages, diagnostic delays, and errors. Depending on where a patient receives a scan, results may take days or even longer to return.

"Whether you are receiving a scan at a larger health system that is facing increasing volume or a rural hospital with limited resources, innovative technologies are needed to improve access to radiology services," said Vikas Gulani, M.D. Ph.D., co-author and chair of the Department of Radiology at U-M Health.

"Our teams at University of Michigan have collaborated to develop a cutting-edge solution to this problem with tremendous, scalable potential."

Although Prima performed strongly, researchers emphasize that the work is still in an early evaluation phase. Future research will focus on incorporating more detailed patient information and electronic medical record data to further improve diagnostic accuracy.

This approach mirrors how radiologists and physicians interpret MRIs and other imaging studies in real clinical settings. While artificial intelligence is already used in health care, most existing systems are limited to narrowly defined tasks.

Hollon describes Prima as "ChatGPT for medical imaging," noting that similar technology could eventually be adapted for other imaging types, including mammograms, chest X-rays and ultrasounds.

"Like the way AI tools can help draft an email or provide recommendations, Prima aims to be a co-pilot for interpreting medical imaging studies," Hollon said.

"We believe that Prima exemplifies the transformative potential of integrating health systems and AI-driven models to improve health care through innovation."

Additional authors: Asadur Chowdury, M.S., Soumyanil Banerjee, M.S., Rachel Gologorsky, Shixuan Liu, Anna-Katharina Meissner, M.D., Akshay Rao, Chenhui Zhao, Akhil Kondepudi, Cheng Jiang, Xinhai Hou, Rushikesh S. Joshi, M.D., Volker Neuschmelting, M.D., Ashok Srinivasan, M.D., Dawn Kleindorfer, M.D., Brian Athey, Ph.D., Aditya Pandey, M.D., and Honglak Lee, Ph.D., all of University of Michigan.

#10882. Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

#10879. Brain inspired machines are better at math than expected

Computers designed to mimic the structure of the human brain are showing an unexpected strength. They can solve some of the demanding mathematical equations that lie at the heart of major scientific and engineering problems.

In a study published in Nature Machine Intelligence, Sandia National Laboratories computational neuroscientists Brad Theilman and Brad Aimone introduced a new algorithm that allows neuromorphic hardware to solve partial differential equations, or PDEs -- the mathematical foundation for modeling phenomena such as fluid dynamics, electromagnetic fields and structural mechanics.

The results demonstrate that neuromorphic systems can handle these equations efficiently. The advance could help open the door to the first neuromorphic supercomputer, offering a new path toward energy efficient computing for national security and other critical applications.

The research was funded by the Department of Energy's Office of Science through the Advanced Scientific Computing Research and Basic Energy Sciences programs, as well as the National Nuclear Security Administration's Advanced Simulation and Computing program.

Solving Partial Differential Equations With Brain Like Hardware

Partial differential equations are essential for simulating real world systems. They are used to forecast weather, analyze how materials respond to stress, and model complex physical processes. Traditionally, solving PDEs requires enormous computing power. Neuromorphic computers approach the problem differently by processing information in ways that resemble how the brain operates.

"We're just starting to have computational systems that can exhibit intelligent-like behavior. But they look nothing like the brain, and the amount of resources that they require is ridiculous, frankly," Theilman said.

For years, neuromorphic systems were mainly viewed as tools for pattern recognition or for speeding up artificial neural networks. Few expected them to manage mathematically rigorous problems such as PDEs, which are typically handled by large scale supercomputers.

Aimone and Theilman were not surprised by the outcome. They argue that the human brain routinely carries out highly complex calculations, even if people are unaware of it.

"Pick any sort of motor control task -- like hitting a tennis ball or swinging a bat at a baseball," Aimone said. "These are very sophisticated computations. They are exascale-level problems that our brains are capable of doing very cheaply."

Energy Efficient Computing for National Security

The findings could have major implications for the National Nuclear Security Administration, which is responsible for maintaining the nation's nuclear deterrent. Supercomputers used across the nuclear weapons complex consume vast amounts of electricity to simulate the physics of nuclear systems and other high stakes scenarios.

Neuromorphic computing may provide a way to significantly cut energy use while still delivering strong computational performance. By solving PDEs in a brain inspired manner, these systems suggest that large simulations could be run using far less power than conventional supercomputers require.

"You can solve real physics problems with brain-like computation," Aimone said. "That's something you wouldn't expect because people's intuition goes the opposite way. And in fact, that intuition is often wrong."

The team envisions neuromorphic supercomputers eventually becoming central to Sandia's mission of protecting national security.

What Neuromorphic Computing Reveals About the Brain

Beyond engineering advances, the research also touches on deeper questions about intelligence and how the brain performs calculations. The algorithm developed by Theilman and Aimone closely mirrors the structure and behavior of cortical networks.

"We based our circuit on a relatively well-known model in the computational neuroscience world," Theilman said. "We've shown the model has a natural but non-obvious link to PDEs, and that link hasn't been made until now -- 12 years after the model was introduced."

The researchers believe this work could help connect neuroscience with applied mathematics, offering new understanding of how the brain processes information.

"Diseases of the brain could be diseases of computation," Aimone said. "But we don't have a solid grasp on how the brain performs computations yet."

If that idea proves correct, neuromorphic computing might one day contribute to better understanding and treatment of neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's.

Building the Next Generation of Supercomputers

Neuromorphic computing remains an emerging field, but this work represents an important step forward. The Sandia team hopes their results will encourage collaboration among mathematicians, neuroscientists and engineers to expand what this technology can achieve.

"If we've already shown that we can import this relatively basic but fundamental applied math algorithm into neuromorphic -- is there a corresponding neuromorphic formulation for even more advanced applied math techniques?" Theilman said.

As development continues, the researchers are optimistic. "We have a foot in the door for understanding the scientific questions, but also we have something that solves a real problem," Theilman said.

#10878. Quantum computer breakthrough tracks qubit fluctuations in real time

Researchers at the Niels Bohr Institute have significantly increased how quickly changes in delicate quantum states can be detected inside a qubit. By combining commercially available hardware with new adaptive measurement techniques, the team can now observe rapid shifts in qubit behavior that were previously impossible to see.

Qubits are the fundamental units of quantum computers, which scientists hope will one day outperform today's most powerful machines. But qubits are extremely sensitive. The materials used to build them often contain tiny defects that scientists still do not fully understand. These microscopic imperfections can shift position hundreds of times per second. As they move, they alter how quickly a qubit loses energy and with it valuable quantum information.

Until recently, standard testing methods took up to a minute to measure qubit performance. That was far too slow to capture these rapid fluctuations. Instead, researchers could only determine an average energy loss rate, masking the true and often unstable behavior of the qubit.

It is somewhat like asking a strong workhorse to pull a plow while obstacles constantly appear in its path faster than anyone can react. The animal may be capable, but unpredictable disruptions make the job much harder.

A research team from the Niels Bohr Institute's Center for Quantum Devices and the Novo Nordisk Foundation Quantum Computing Programme, led by postdoctoral researcher Dr. Fabrizio Berritta, developed a real time adaptive measurement system that tracks changes in the qubit energy loss (relaxation) rate as they occur. The project involved collaboration with scientists from the Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leiden University, and Chalmers University.

The new approach relies on a fast classical controller that updates its estimate of a qubit's relaxation rate within milliseconds. This matches the natural speed of the fluctuations themselves, rather than lagging seconds or minutes behind as older methods did.

To achieve this, the team used a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a type of classical processor designed for extremely rapid operations. By running the experiment directly on the FPGA, they could quickly generate a "best guess" of how fast the qubit was losing energy using only a few measurements. This eliminated the need for slower data transfers to a conventional computer.

Programming FPGAs for such specialized tasks can be challenging. Even so, the researchers succeeded in updating the controller's internal Bayesian model after every single qubit measurement. That allowed the system to continually refine its understanding of the qubit's condition in real time.

As a result, the controller now keeps pace with the qubit's changing environment. Measurements and adjustments happen on nearly the same timescale as the fluctuations themselves, making the system roughly one hundred times faster than previously demonstrated.

The work also revealed something new. Scientists did not previously know just how quickly fluctuations occur in superconducting qubits. These experiments have now provided that insight.

Commercial Quantum Hardware Meets Advanced Control

FPGAs have long been used in other scientific and engineering fields. In this case, the researchers used a commercially available FPGA based controller from Quantum Machines called the OPX1000. The system can be programmed in a language similar to Python, which many physicists already use, making it more accessible to research groups worldwide.

The integration of this controller with advanced quantum hardware was made possible through close collaboration between the Niels Bohr Institute research group led by Associate Professor Morten Kjaergaard and Chalmers University, where the quantum processing unit was designed and fabricated. "The controller enables very tight integration between logic, measurements and feedforward: these components made our experiment possible," says Morten Kjærgaard.

Why Real Time Calibration Matters for Quantum Computers

Quantum technologies promise powerful new capabilities, though practical large scale quantum computers are still under development. Progress often comes incrementally, but occasionally major steps forward occur.

By uncovering these previously hidden dynamics, the findings reshape how scientists think about testing and calibrating superconducting quantum processors. With current materials and manufacturing methods, moving toward real time monitoring and adjustment appears essential for improving reliability. The results also highlight the importance of partnerships between academic research and industry, along with creative uses of available technology.

"Nowadays, in quantum processing units in general, the overall performance is not determined by the best qubits, but by the worst ones: those are the ones we need to focus on. The surprise from our work is that a 'good' qubit can turn into a 'bad' one in fractions of a second, rather than minutes or hours.

"With our algorithm, the fast control hardware can pinpoint which qubit is 'good' or 'bad' basically in real time. We can also gather useful statistics on the 'bad` qubits in seconds instead of hours or days.

"We still cannot explain a large fraction of the fluctuations we observe. Understanding and controlling the physics behind such fluctuations in qubit properties will be necessary for scaling quantum processors to a useful size," Fabrizio says.

Materials provided by University of Copenhagen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10877. Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.
