#10875. 6 Best Coding Apps for Beginners to Learn Programming

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning to code can feel overwhelming at first. There are different programming languages, development tools, and concepts to understand, and choosing where to begin is not always easy. Coding apps can make the process more approachable by breaking lessons into smaller steps and giving beginners a place to practice what they learn.

Whether you want to build websites, develop software, or explore programming as a new skill, the right app can help you get started. Some platforms focus on interactive lessons, while others offer coding exercises, guided projects, or access to a full development environment.

Here are six coding apps worth considering if you're learning programming for the first time.

Mimo is a beginner-friendly coding app designed to help users learn programming through short lessons and interactive exercises. Rather than starting with lengthy explanations, it introduces concepts in manageable steps, making it easier to fit learning into a busy schedule.

The app covers several topics, including Python, JavaScript, HTML, CSS, and SQL. This makes it useful for beginners who want to explore general programming or focus on web development. As learners progress, they can work through structured learning paths and practice applying concepts through coding exercises and projects.

Mimo is particularly suitable for people who prefer a guided learning experience instead of figuring out what to study next on their own. Its mobile-first approach also makes it convenient to practice during breaks or while traveling.

A free starting option is available, although access to some courses and features requires a paid plan.

Best for: Beginners who want structured lessons and practical coding exercises.

SoloLearn is a coding education platform that helps beginners explore programming languages through short lessons, quizzes, and hands-on practice. It is a useful starting point for people who want to understand programming fundamentals before committing to a specific development path.

The platform offers courses in languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, Java, C++, SQL, HTML, and CSS. Its bite-sized lessons make complicated topics easier to approach, while interactive exercises let learners test their understanding as they progress.

Another useful feature is its learning community, where users can explore other people's code, participate in discussions, and learn from different approaches to programming problems.

SoloLearn works well for people who are still deciding which language to learn. However, completing lessons alone is not enough to develop strong programming skills. Beginners should also practice writing their own programs and building small projects.

The platform offers free learning options alongside paid features.

Best for: Beginners who want to explore multiple programming languages and learn through short, interactive lessons.

Codecademy Go is the mobile companion to Codecademy's online learning platform. It is designed to help learners reinforce programming concepts and review material while away from their computers.

Codecademy's broader learning platform offers courses covering web development, Python, JavaScript, SQL, and other technical subjects. The mobile app can be useful for reviewing concepts, practicing through supported activities, and maintaining a consistent learning routine between longer study sessions.

For example, someone taking a JavaScript course on a computer can use the mobile experience to revisit concepts and strengthen their understanding before returning to practical exercises.

It is important to distinguish between learning on a mobile device and developing software on one. Codecademy Go is primarily a companion for learning and review rather than a complete replacement for a desktop coding environment.

Some learning content is available without a subscription, while access to additional courses and features depends on the current plan.

Best for: Learners who want to review programming concepts alongside a structured online course.

Programming Hub is an educational app for people who want to explore programming and related technical subjects in one place. It brings together lessons, examples, and exercises designed to introduce users to different areas of software development.

Its learning material covers languages such as Python, Java, C++, and JavaScript, along with web development and other computing topics. This variety can be helpful for beginners who want to compare different languages before choosing one to study in depth.

The app's lesson-based approach makes it possible to explore a topic without immediately installing a development environment or configuring complicated software. Code examples and practice activities can also help learners connect theoretical concepts with practical applications.

However, beginners should avoid jumping between too many languages at once. Starting with one language, understanding its fundamentals, and applying those skills to a small project will generally provide a stronger foundation.

Programming Hub offers free and paid options, with access depending on the selected plan.

Best for: Beginners who want an introduction to several programming languages and computing topics.

Enki takes a slightly different approach to learning programming by emphasizing regular practice and the development of technical skills over time. Its lessons and exercises are designed to help users strengthen their understanding through consistent, manageable learning sessions.

Depending on the learning content available, users can explore topics such as Python, SQL, JavaScript, data analysis, and other areas of technology. This makes the app relevant to both aspiring developers and people who want to improve their technical knowledge for work.

One advantage of short, recurring practice sessions is that they encourage learners to revisit concepts instead of studying them once and moving on. Regular revision can help reinforce programming syntax, terminology, and problem-solving techniques.

Enki may be especially useful for people who already have some exposure to coding and want to maintain a learning habit. Complete beginners should check whether the available lessons match their chosen language and current skill level before starting.

The app offers different access options, so check its current listing for details about free and paid features.

Best for: Learners who prefer regular practice and gradual improvement in technical skills.

Replit is a browser-based development platform that allows users to write, run, and test code without setting up a traditional development environment on their computer. It is particularly useful for beginners who want to move beyond lessons and start creating their own programs.

Users can work with languages and technologies such as Python, JavaScript, HTML, and CSS, depending on the project and environment. Instead of only answering quiz questions, learners can experiment with code, identify errors, and see how their changes affect a working application.

For example, a beginner learning Python could create a simple calculator, while someone studying web development could build a basic personal website. Starting with small projects helps learners understand how individual programming concepts work together.

Replit also provides AI-assisted development features, which can help users explore ideas and understand code. However, beginners should review generated code and learn why it works rather than relying on automated suggestions without understanding them.

Replit offers a free starting option, with additional capabilities available through paid plans. Features and usage limits may vary.

Best for: Beginners who want to practice writing code and experiment with real projects in a development environment.

The best coding app depends on your learning goals, preferred learning style, and current experience. Before choosing one, consider what you want to accomplish.

Choose a language first. If you want to learn general programming, Python is a reasonable starting point. If your goal is to build websites, HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are more directly relevant.

Consider how you learn. Apps such as Mimo and SoloLearn emphasize short lessons and interactive practice. Codecademy Go is useful for reviewing course material, while Replit provides an environment for experimenting with code.

Look for practical exercises. Understanding a lesson is only the beginning. Choose resources that encourage you to write code, solve problems, and apply concepts in small projects.

Check the cost. Several coding apps provide free introductory content, but some courses and advanced features may require subscriptions. Review the current pricing before committing to a paid plan.

Move beyond the app. As your skills improve, practice using a computer, read technical documentation, and create projects of your own. These activities help you develop the problem-solving skills required for real programming work.

Coding apps provide an accessible way to begin learning programming without needing extensive technical knowledge or complicated software installations. They can help you understand basic concepts, practice regularly, and discover which areas of development interest you most.

Mimo and SoloLearn are worth exploring if you prefer guided lessons, while Codecademy Go can complement a structured course. Programming Hub and Enki offer alternative approaches to learning and practice, and Replit provides a way to experiment with working code.

You do not need to master every programming language or use several apps simultaneously. Start with one resource, focus on a single learning goal, and gradually apply what you learn to practical projects. Consistent practice and curiosity will take you further than simply completing lessons.

#10873. ChatGPT as a therapist? New study reveals serious ethical risks

As more people seek mental health advice from ChatGPT and other large language models (LLMs), new research suggests these AI chatbots may not be ready for that role. The study found that even when instructed to use established psychotherapy approaches, the systems consistently fail to meet professional ethics standards set by organizations such as the American Psychological Association.

Researchers from Brown University, working closely with mental health professionals, identified repeated patterns of problematic behavior. In testing, chatbots mishandled crisis situations, gave responses that reinforced harmful beliefs about users or others, and used language that created the appearance of empathy without genuine understanding.

"In this work, we present a practitioner-informed framework of 15 ethical risks to demonstrate how LLM counselors violate ethical standards in mental health practice by mapping the model's behavior to specific ethical violations," the researchers wrote in their study. "We call on future work to create ethical, educational and legal standards for LLM counselors -- standards that are reflective of the quality and rigor of care required for human-facilitated psychotherapy."

The findings were presented at the AAAI/ACM Conference on Artificial Intelligence, Ethics and Society. The research team is affiliated with Brown's Center for Technological Responsibility, Reimagination and Redesign.

Zainab Iftikhar, a Ph.D. candidate in computer science at Brown who led the study, set out to examine whether carefully worded prompts could guide AI systems to behave more ethically in mental health settings. Prompts are written instructions designed to steer a model's output without retraining it or adding new data.

"Prompts are instructions that are given to the model to guide its behavior for achieving a specific task," Iftikhar said. "You don't change the underlying model or provide new data, but the prompt helps guide the model's output based on its pre-existing knowledge and learned patterns.

"For example, a user might prompt the model with: 'Act as a cognitive behavioral therapist to help me reframe my thoughts,' or 'Use principles of dialectical behavior therapy to assist me in understanding and managing my emotions.' While these models do not actually perform these therapeutic techniques like a human would, they rather use their learned patterns to generate responses that align with the concepts of CBT or DBT based on the input prompt provided."

People regularly share these prompt strategies on platforms like TikTok, Instagram, and Reddit. Beyond individual experimentation, many consumer facing mental health chatbots are built by applying therapy related prompts to general purpose LLMs. That makes it especially important to understand whether prompting alone can make AI counseling safer.

Testing AI Chatbots in Simulated Counseling

To evaluate the systems, the researchers observed seven trained peer counselors who had experience with cognitive behavioral therapy. These counselors conducted self counseling sessions with AI models prompted to act as CBT therapists. The models tested included versions of OpenAI's GPT Series, Anthropic's Claude, and Meta's Llama.

The team then selected simulated chats based on real human counseling conversations. Three licensed clinical psychologists reviewed those transcripts to flag possible ethical violations.

The analysis uncovered 15 distinct risks grouped into five broad categories:

The Accountability Gap in AI Mental Health

Iftikhar noted that human therapists can also make mistakes. The key difference is oversight.

"For human therapists, there are governing boards and mechanisms for providers to be held professionally liable for mistreatment and malpractice," Iftikhar said. "But when LLM counselors make these violations, there are no established regulatory frameworks."

The researchers emphasize that their findings do not suggest AI has no place in mental health care. Tools powered by artificial intelligence could help expand access, particularly for people who face high costs or limited availability of licensed professionals. However, the study highlights the need for clear safeguards, responsible deployment, and stronger regulatory structures before relying on these systems in high stakes situations.

For now, Iftikhar hopes the work encourages caution.

"If you're talking to a chatbot about mental health, these are some things that people should be looking out for," she said.

Ellie Pavlick, a Brown computer science professor who was not involved in the research, said the study underscores the importance of carefully examining AI systems used in sensitive areas like mental health. Pavlick leads ARIA, a National Science Foundation AI research institute at Brown focused on building trustworthy AI assistants.

"The reality of AI today is that it's far easier to build and deploy systems than to evaluate and understand them," Pavlick said. "This paper required a team of clinical experts and a study that lasted for more than a year in order to demonstrate these risks. Most work in AI today is evaluated using automatic metrics which, by design, are static and lack a human in the loop."

She added that the study could serve as a model for future research aimed at improving safety in AI mental health tools.

"There is a real opportunity for AI to play a role in combating the mental health crisis that our society is facing, but it's of the utmost importance that we take the time to really critique and evaluate our systems every step of the way to avoid doing more harm than good," Pavlick said. "This work offers a good example of what that can look like."

#10871. What we've been playing - "I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences for weeks"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Happy Halloween! Are you scared? I am: I'm in a theatre play that opens next week and we're not ready yet. I'm also off to a ghost stories evening at the theatre tonight; I've had a few people there tell me, with faces as straight as a ruler, of their eerie encounters. Actually, one encounter involved me.

I was in a two-hander there, a play with only two people in it, and I got to the changing room one evening to see a concerned look on the face of the other person in the show. "Have you only just got here?" he asked. "Yes," I said in response. And at this, he blanched. He's a gruff sort of fellow with a streetwise sensibility: not a person I would associate with being easily scared. But he was. "I was just talking to you," he said. "You made the curtain move as you walked past." I remember the hairs on my arms and neck standing up, and I haven't been as confident sitting in that dressing room alone since.

But back to games! This, as you know, is our regular feature where we talk about what we've been playing this week. This week, Marie cooks with Death; Connor revisits a game that terrified him as a teenager; and Bertie is a little bit disappointed by a new indie horror.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago (it happens). And now onward!

My supernatural farm is coming together very slowly, and I do mean slowly. I'm not in a rush this time - my many restarts of Stardew Valley have taught me to be patient while building or I'll want to wipe everything and start afresh. Also, I do not do scary games in the slightest, so this is my version of a halloween-themed entry, and I am fine with that.

So: my current farm contains a small livestock barn, a vegetable patch, a rather fetching vineyard next to some kegs, and I've just started to renovate the lower level by the beach. What that will be is yet to be decided, but the slow progress isn't bothering me as much as I thought it would. In fact, being unable to race through it is making it more enjoyable.

Also, watching Death learning to cook was not on my 2026 bingo card, but I am adamant a friendship will form from the literal ashes of his creations.

Given we're in the Halloween season and the site has been diving into all things scary, I wanted to pop back and play the game that kicked off a horror revival of sorts when I was almost a teenager: Amnesia!

Like many my age, my first exposure to Amnesia was through YouTube playthroughs back in the Let's Play early years. I didn't actually touch it myself until years later, dipping my toes in the game and swiftly pulling them back out, frightened.

Now I'm older and hardened by adult life, I wanted to give it another go and see how horror games have evolved over time. While they certainly have come a way since 2010, Amnesia holds up! It's well worth playing if you want a blast from the past.

I'm a bit disappointed. I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences from Tenebris Somnia for weeks, anticipating what I believed would be a terrifying experience. But a couple of hours into the game, I haven't had that. I've written about this at more length in a Tenebris Somnia preview, but suffice to say the adventure game half of Tenebris Somnia lets it down a bit, I think. However, I'm still impressed; I'm impressed by people approaching horror - and games - in these interesting ways.

It also reminds me strongly of Faith: The Unholy Trilogy, which is another, closely related 8-bit horror game that really is terrifying. It's tighter and meaner, and its crazed, hand-drawn MS Paint cinematics are worth seeing up close for yourself.

What a spooky week it's been! After Monday's trawl into some of our favourite scary games, I delved - some 30 years after my original encounter - back into pioneering survival horror Ecstatica, dongs and all. Since then, we've had post-Soviet grimness, minty fresh breath and misery, plus Bertie straddling the line between retro pixel art and retro FMV.

In amongst all that (and a bit of Silver Pines), I've squeaked in some time with The Killing Stone. It's very early days, but I'm already intrigued by its distinctive mix of roguelike deck-builder and first-person narrative horror, especially given the folkloric aspects of it all as you, a Scandinavian witch's apprentice, reckon with previous dealings with the devil.

It's fascinating so far, with an unusual card-based combat system that plays out across a single line, meaning placement matters as you attempt to snuff out your opponent's orb at the far end by battling through - or around - its guards. Each of your units can be augmented with spells and abilities placed strategically, and adjacently, outside of the battlefield, introducing even more possibilities. And I especially love the fact you can play the whole thing in modern or ye olde English, the strong voice cast making fine work of both.

I'm excited to explore more, then, but with Halloween here I've other engagements to see through first. Two annual movie traditions: Trick 'r Treat, and then - as someone old enough to have been scarred by it the first time around - controversial BBC classic Ghostwatch, with Michael Parkinson, Craig Charles, and Sarah Greene. Over three decades later it remains remarkably effective, and that bedroom Pipes reveal still haunts me.

#10869. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10867. Silver Pines is a great survival horror throwback with an unexpected Metroidvania twist, but it makes me glad the genre's moved on

It's those bloody typewriters all over again. Developer Wych Elm Games' Silver Pines is fantastic in a whole bunch of ways, from its woozy small-town ambience to its mix of taut survival horror and exploratory Metroidvania. But from the very first moment I saw its save system, its please-insert-coin phone box demands, I knew our relationship was going to be a fractious one.

Silver Pines begins, however, in the gloom of a 1950s diner; lights off, windows boarded, as the titular town awaits an oncoming storm. You are private investigator Red Walker, but your jittery demeanour and Valium drawl suggests you're equally oblivious to the whos, whats, and whys of it all. Out in the parking lot, a phone box rings; there's cryptic talk of a missing musician and a motel room. Then, the scene set, it's onward into the heart of the town.

Silver Pines dabbles in neo-noir and psychological horror, but its debt to David Lynch - Twin Peaks in particular - is unmistakable. If the 50s diner opener wasn't enough of a tip-off, it's not long before the town's cast of beautifully rotoscoped oddballs begin making themselves known; enigmatic conversations unfurling in awkward non-sequiters. Then later: as Red passes through a suspicious door, reality seems to bend entirely, taking him to a dingy nightclub bar somewhere out of time. There's no talking giant, but we do get a song.

Twin Peaks' iconography has been borrowed and repackaged so much in the 36 years since its landmark arrival, it's hard to pay it homage without ringing the creative death knell. So it's testament to Silver Pines that, for all its hat-tips, it still manages to find a mood that feels like its own. Granted, its one that exists at the intersection David Lynch and Silent Hill (itself indebted to Twin Peaks) but the town's ambience - a kind of dishevelled melancholy - is an affecting one. Its world, built from layers upon layers of strikingly painterly 2D artwork, is drab and desolate; perpetually dank and lost in artfully controlled shadow. Its sorrowful monstrosities shimmer like a suggestion. And beneath the wind and the rain and the wailing of lost souls lies an ambient synthline so slight the soundscape skirts close to ASMR.

That, though, is entirely fitting for an adventure whose pace is such a languid one; as woozily unfocused as Red seems to be when he first picks up that phone. That's not a criticism though. Silver Pines isn't afraid to create space for its insidious ambience to slither in. There are pockets of staccato violence, absolutely, but between the deliberately stilted gunplay and awkwardly stumbling melee its primary rhythms are exploratory ones.

Which is where the Metroidvania stylings come in. Silver Pines, the town, is a vast, interconnected space: drizzly exteriors lead to gloomy interiors that branch out to new pathways or double back to previously impassable ones (you'll be hearing Red muttering "The door opens from the other side!" in your dreams). Sometimes you'll stumble across a perfectory puzzle, but progress most often requires identifying which part of the map, perhaps a location first seen much earlier, might succumb to new tools: a boxcutter that opens up passageways sealed with plastic; a pistol that can shatter rusty padlocks. And a shout-out to one of my favourite underused Metroidvania mechanics - the ability to photograph points of interest and pin them to your map, serving as reminders for future you.

It all makes for a haltingly ambulatory flow that suits and serves the quieter mood. But truthfully, having hit the five-hour mark, I'm finding the whole thing a bit of a struggle as I grapple with its intentional throwback design. Beneath Silver Pines' sumptuously bleak presentation lies an ancient heart. And while I'm certainly not criticising a deliberately old-school adventure for playing it old-school, it is making me thankful the genre's moved on.

Silver Pines, like most survival horror, builds tension through scarcity and impermanence, offering thrilling glimpses of power only to take them all away again: bullets are limited, melee weapons break, and the resources needed to restore them are equally few. Without all that, your only hope of survival is a stumbling duck-and-weave. But Silver Pines spins a particularly punishing web, tying everything to the finite number of coins left out in the world. Ammo, duct tape, health items, and so on are rare finds on the map, but comparatively plentiful in vending machines. However, coins are also the currency you'll need for phone box saves, meaning every choice you make feels even more perilous; the perpetual threat of a death spiral.

And the truth is, as much as I love survival horror, I've always hated limited saves; not just the push-your-luck stresses they invoke as I'm forced to decide if I dare risk - for instance - my last Resident Evil typewriter ribbon, but the steadily deflating atmosphere when my luck runs out and I've got to do the same section all over again. It's a problem modern survival horror still grapples with, of course, but, by jettisoning the arbitrary restrictions of limited save currency, they're able to manage a more sustained momentum.

And by binding its survival systems so tightly - the threat of weapons failure, the threat of death - under the guise of coins, Silver Pines has made me even more obsessive about my saves, even more cautious about frivolous spending. And it's turned mood into mechanisms. I'm min-max route plotting, enemy tracking, bullet counting, and limiting my box cutter swings - so much energy spent on mental spreadsheet-building I'm no longer immersed in the mystery of the thing. And the open-ended structure does rather exacerbate the issue: its long, slow stretches of nothing followed by unexpected deaths make redoing bits particularly wearying.

Of course, it's not Silver Pines' fault it's igniting my old-school survival horror PTSD. And I'm fully aware there are sickos out there who love this kind of thing; the queasily unshakeable threat of spiralling disaster and the harsh punishment when calculations are off and disaster finally comes. But you know what? I certainly respect Silver Pines, even if we don't always get along. It's a gorgeously woozy exercise in mood, with a fishing line mystery that - for all my grumbles - just keeps pulling me on. Not for nothing is its enigmatic bar known as The Rabbit Hole, and as the search for Eddie Velvet continues, here I am; lost in the warrens again.

#10866. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

#10863. The Mortuary Assistant dev on laying his cancelled Paranormal Activity game to rest, resurrecting it as a Halloween freebie, and a potential return to his biggest game

"[It] makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

Halloween feels like an especially appropriate time for Playable Archive's arrival. It's a genuinely spooky found-footage affair for one - a cleverly orchestrated 30-or-so minutes of scares from the developer of horror hit The Mortuary Assistant, released this week as a Halloween freebie. But more than that, the unusual tale of its creation is also one of death and resurrection. As its slightly cheeky title implies, Playable Archive (a riff on the legendary Playable Teaser created for Hideo Kojima's cancelled Silent Hill game) is a small slice of developer Brian Clarke's much larger cancelled Paranormal Activity game, making it a fascinating window into 'what could have been'. When I heard the news of its release, my curiosity was piqued, so I spoke with Clarke about this unexpected turn of events, of laying his project to rest, and how its legacy might live on - perhaps even as a new Mortuary Assistant game.

Playable Archive's story begins sometime after the launch of The Mortuary Assistant in 2022, when Clarke was approached by publisher DreadXP to gauge his interest in developing a game set in the universe of the Paranormal Activity movies. "I was like, sure, I'd be down to do that," Clarke tells me over video call, "because I love Paranormal Activity. It's one of my favourite horror films." One thing led to another and Clarke found himself submitting a design and pitch deck to Paramount. And the company clearly liked it; amid a major management shakeup at DreadXP, the "slow process of contracts" began, and the game would eventually be announced as Paranormal Activity: Threshold in November last year.

For Clarke, Paranormal Activity was a good fit for his preferred story-first approach to horror: one less about cinematic scares that seize control of the camera, and more about "passive" chills; moments where something happens which might not be obvious, and which many players might even miss, giving them slightly different experiences they'll have fun sharing. "I think the flavour I like to do," he explains, "is something that feels like you're driving the experience as opposed to me just putting you on rails and showing you."

And the project felt like an opportunity to add depth to a series that so far hasn't delved much further than spooky houses and demonic possession, in a way Clarke particularly enjoys. "I love lore - demonic lore was a big thing in The Mortuary Assistant - [and so] I built a whole demonology for Paranormal Activity, and built out this whole new concept that still tied in with everything from the films," he recalls. "I was really excited to do this deeper thing ... that maybe could be used for other things in the future, other games, films, you know."

The vestiges of Clarke's original pitch and his broader ambitions can still be seen in Playable Archive. His take on Paranormal Activity would follow a couple as they moved into a new home - presented using the movie series' familiar found-footage style - with events gradually taking a supernatural turn. Except, at a key point, the perspective would shift, and we'd learn we're watching this old camcorder footage through the eyes of a detective, investigating the house following the couple's unexplained demise. In the planned full-length version of the game, the action would jump between the two timelines as more tapes are found, and information learned in one time period would help players progress in the other. However, a third perspective would reveal "something has been set in motion outside of these two".

Clarke, who works as a solo developer under his DarkStone Digital label, had already spent around two years developing the Paranormal Activity project before it made its PAX East debut earlier this year. And the feedback to his PAX demo - more or less the exact same slice that's now been tidied up for public release as Playable Archive - was extremely positive. But things were about to take a turn. With Paramount's deadline approaching, it was clear there was still work to do, and Clarke requested a "small extension". Not just to finish the game, but to complete QA, localisation, and porting to get Paranormal Activity ready for a simultaneous release. But Paramount, perhaps eager for a Halloween release this year, said no.

"It was really a stressful time because I had put a lot of pressure on myself for the project and I was pouring a lot of myself into it," Clarke recalls, noting that, for him, quality is everything - "I feel it shows when people play any of my projects". With the likes of additional mo-cap, voice acting, and editing work still to be done in order to complement Paranormal Activity's completed mechanics, he was concerned rushing to meet Paramount's inflexible deadline would leave the second half lacking after investing so much time in the earlier part of the game. "That sort of thing … feels much more damaging to an individual [developer's reputation] than a big old company," he says, and so the idea of putting "something out faster, and very much compromising what the game is going to be" wasn't something Clarke wanted to entertain: "I talked to Dread … and we all kind of agreed that, yeah, if we can't [get Paramount to] budge then we'll just have to kind of end it.

"To Paramount's credit, when … we floated [the idea of ending things] they were okay with that." But notably, Clarke was permitted to keep everything he'd created for the project, which is why it's been possible to trim out the Paranormal Activity branding and release Playable Archive this Halloween. It was current DreadXP director Hunter Bond who first suggested giving the project a public send-off of sorts, according to Clarke, so it could "sort of get its flowers [and] be laid to rest". For Clarke, who was in triple-A games development for 13 years before going independent, game cancellations were far from unfamiliar. And while "it's harder when it's just my work" he felt he'd "accepted that, okay, this one's not done." But it wasn't just him: "It seemed like [the project's cancellation] was probably sitting with various people at Dread" and so the decision was made to let it live on in some small way. "And now that it's out there," says Clarke, "it feels really good to let people actually play it."

Playable Archive packs a surprising amount of tension and some killer scares into its scant 30-45 minutes of playtime, but there wasn't really ever any thought of charging for it. "Just knowing it's not the full game [it was intended to be], "explains Clarke, "I can't bring myself to be like, 'Hey, give me money for that.' It's been four years since The Mortuary Assistant came out [and] I would love to put something out that could bring a little something in, but it's just not the piece for it. And Dread felt the same way."

Playable Archive isn't quite the same experience PAX East attendees had with the Paranormal Activity demo earlier this year, though. As well as scrubbing the branding, Clarke took the opportunity to make a number of adjustments, even adding some new lore. At one point, he considered expanding it still further, incorporating found-footage segments from the larger game. In the end, though, he decided there was "no elegant way" to do it.

"I'd want to be like, no, you need to access this to use the demonic connection, and to open this," he elaborates. "And then if you access this, well, this room's art isn't finished so I'd have to finish that. So it turned into a big old can of worms. Like, if I wanted to do more - which I would have loved to have done - it just would have been a bigger time investment, at which point I might as well be trying to finish the game … So ultimately [we decided] let's just polish up and make this demo the experience so people can at least see where I was going with it and get something out of it. It's better than letting it collect dust in the dark."

But there's that elephant in the room: why not finish what he started and release the full game? The answer, Clark suggests, is a little complicated. There are the technical practicalities for one - "What I created was very much meant to tie into Paranormal Activity and so to go back and adjust all of that would be a tonne of work," Clarke explains - but there's the personal aspect too; of resurrecting a game he'd already put to rest after two years of development, and of potentially being drawn back into rhythm - "inviting an unnatural process to my normal process" - he'd already struggled with the first time around. "There're a lot of factors," says Clarke, "beyond just 'Brian doesn't want to do it because he's sad', right?"

And in the end, Clarke found himself asking a simple question: "Is it better to take the concepts that I really like, that are mine and that I got to keep, and make new stuff with it?" That, ultimately, is what felt "more valuable" to him, and since reaching that decision, he's "come up with like five different game ideas, so I'd much rather chase those."

Clarke admits, though, that he's kept a curious eye on Playable Archive's reception, and says the hugely positive response has been "beautiful" to see. "When this much time passes," he says, "I think it's really common for artists and creatives to have impostor syndrome and so it's nice to be like, 'Okay, I still know what I'm doing to some degree, right?'" But with the positive feedback comes the inevitable calls to finish the game. "And I've got to fight that urge," says Clarke, "[but] I see people play through the whole thing again and I go, 'Man, that was a good story.' Like, I know where I was going with it. I know what I wanted to do with it, and people were kind of starting to understand what I was doing."

But despite that lingering temptation, Clarke seems eager to move on, and is already juggling a number of ideas that draw on both The Mortuary Assistant and Paranormal Activity. "I've realised I've got so much here," he says. "What could I build with these? I don't want to build Playable Archive again because that exists … but can I take elements from this?" According to Clarke, there are "two games in particular" he wants to do, that he thinks will strike a chord with players, and that feature concepts recognisable from Playable Archive. "I'm going to execute them again, but with more focus on those things," he explains, pointing to Playable Archive's Ouija board and different timelines. "Those things will live on. So this whole thing, at this point, makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

For Clarke, the positive response to Playable Archive is "bittersweet", but it's also "really been motivating. It's really made me be like, alright, it's time to roll up and do this again. After everything happened … I'm out on the other side and I want to make something." As for what that something might be, Clarke says he's "already started" work on a new Mortuary Assistant game, and is also tinkering around with a "little prototype" incorporating some of Playable Archive's mechanics. Both, he says, are proving exciting. "This is the problem with the early part of game development: everything is fun. Everything's grey-box. Nothing's anything. It's just ideas. It's really cool … So I think what I need is to get a few ideas out [and decide] do I want to pursue this or do I want to pursue this other idea? You know, whether it's Mortuary Assistant, or this other prototype, or this other [thing]."

Clarke is, though, acuately aware it's been four years since he last put out a full game. "I desperately want to make something full, get it out there, and have people playing it," he says. "So, I'm certainly motivated to have something out soon."

#10862. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

#10860. Microsoft says it now sucks less to use a Windows PC with 8GB of RAM

Using a computer with 8GB of RAM in 2026 is rough, to say the least, but as RAMageddon rages on, Microsoft says it is making improvements to using a Windows PC with less memory available.

In a blog post this week, Microsoft says that Windows PCs with 8GB of RAM are now 20% more responsive and 40% faster (in multitasking) in lab testing.

These are just a few of the updates Microsoft says it has made, with the company still working to get more out of memory compression and work with third-party developers to improve memory usage.

With RAMageddon’s effects only set to get worse in 2027 and problems thought to continue through 2030, this is all good news for end users. It’s also good seeing as Apple’s MacBook Neo triggered an avalanche of new 8GB Windows laptops designed to rival the lower price point.

#10859. AI, DevOps and the Cost of Hosting: From Idea to Production for $0

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI coding agent rebuilt my photo-collage app as a distributed system and deployed it to a free server. The hard part turned out to be hardware: one free ARM CPU, 538 collages created during load testing, zero failed jobs, and $0 a month.

Like most developers, I have never been short of product ideas. Hosting them has always been the problem. I have tried shared hosting with SmartWeb, Azure free credits, Google Compute Engine’s free VM instances, SmartWeb’s virtual private servers, and Heroku dynos. None of them left me satisfied. The worst experience was a $200 charge for an AI address API on Google Cloud—for an application that never earned me a single kobo.

I am not a cheapskate, but I learned quickly that one of the easiest ways to kill a potentially good idea is to get the finances wrong. An application has to stay online until it reaches a critical mass of users, and hosting an untested, unverified product on expensive hardware can be wasteful.

Agentic AI coding has changed how quickly I can move from idea to production. Over about five weeks of intermittent coding—amounting to roughly three days of full work with Claude—my photo-stitching app went from an Angular 8 project that would not even build to a distributed system running in production.

Along the way, Claude upgraded the front end from Angular 8 to Angular 22 and fixed the build on Node 24. It set up RabbitMQ with Docker Compose so the message broker configures itself on startup, and added real-time notifications over SignalR with a progress spinner. It fixed a crash loop caused by an unreadable iPhone photo. It redesigned the system so any server can process any job, with switchable database and file storage, direct browser-to-storage uploads, and photos resized in the browser before upload. It added reliability work that a team might debate for a sprint: a dead-letter queue, confirmed publishing, idempotent workers, recovery of stuck jobs, and automatic clean-up. It fixed the stitching so photos keep their proportions and borders are even. It wrote 41 automated tests and scrubbed leaked passwords and personal photos from the Git history.

Coding, QA, DevOps, and production support used to be separate phases, often handled by separate people. Here they happened in one conversation. When code becomes this cheap to produce, the bottleneck moves. For me, it moved to hardware.

To check what the AI builds, you have to run it locally. A distributed app means running the whole system locally: a web app, a background worker, a message broker, a database, and storage emulators. Because Claude adds features quickly, and each one can add another container, it is easy to exhaust a laptop’s RAM and CPU.

My setup made this lesson concrete. I work on a MacBook Air, with Visual Studio in a Windows virtual machine. Docker cannot run inside that VM on Apple Silicon, so Claude ran Docker on the Mac and drove it from Windows over SSH. Later, my mobile connection started dropping large packets and broke uploads from the VM, so Claude moved the load testing onto the server itself. The software problems took minutes. The hardware and network problems took longer.

Then comes hosting. Heroku looked like the easy route, but it has had no free dynos since 2022. The cheapest option is an Eco dyno at about $5 a month, plus about $5 for Postgres. That is affordable, but running it for an idea that has no users yet did not seem prudent.

Claude suggested Oracle Cloud’s Always Free tier: an ARM server with up to 4 CPUs and 24 GB of RAM, free with no end date. I created a small instance with 1 CPU and about 6 GB. Claude wrote a production Docker Compose file and a one-command setup script: Caddy as the gateway, the web app, the stitching worker, RabbitMQ, and PostgreSQL, with data that survives restarts. My repository is private, so rather than put a GitHub token on a public server, it copied the code over SSH and deployed it. The last blocker was mine to fix: opening ports 80 and 443 in Oracle’s network firewall. Then the site was live.

I did not want to guess, so I asked Claude to load-test the live server. Simulated users ran the full flow: upload two photos, wait for the real-time notification, and download the stitched collage.

The server handled 64 people creating collages at the same moment without a single failed job: 538 collages, zero errors. Its ceiling was about 62 collages a minute—one per second. Beyond that, users queue. With 4 people creating at once, 95% of collages arrived within about five seconds; at 64, each person waited about a minute. If each active user makes a collage every couple of minutes, one CPU comfortably serves around 85 people at once, and a few hundred if they use it less often.

The test also showed what runs out first, and it is not the CPU. Each collage stores about 5 MB, mostly the PNG output, so at full speed the disk would fill in under two hours. Switching to JPEG and shortening retention fixes that, and when traffic grows, three more free CPUs are waiting. That is the value of measuring before paying: I know what to change, and when.

Oracle isn’t being charitable. Servers need applications to run—ideally ones that go viral—and the free tier is a play for loyalty. Oracle wants successful projects to stay and grow into its paid services instead of moving off the platform once they reach critical mass. It can also reclaim free instances that sit idle.

I think the bet is smart, and it points to a bigger trend. AI-generated code still needs energy to run and hardware to host it, even when far fewer people work on each project. As more people turn ideas into working software, demand for compute will keep growing, and I expect hosting and data centres to become a very profitable business.

For now, the combination works: Claude orchestrates the coding, QA, and DevOps, and Oracle’s free server keeps an idea online long enough to find out whether it deserves to grow.

#10858. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (2026) review

I have a friend who first played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time back in the day, when the game was only just released and he was home from university for Christmas. This is objectively the best of all times to play any video game: not Christmas specifically, but dropped out of the regular world and faced with a few weeks filled with nothing pressing to do whatsoever. Years later, though, my friend told me that the magic of the game didn't just come down to the playing of it, but the way that the playing of it unfolded.

Here's what he meant. He'd play all day, curtains drawn, family ignored, working through dungeons and pelting across Hyrule Field on Epona. Then in the evening he'd call his girlfriend, who was back with her own family for Christmas. She always wanted to know what he'd got up to that day. And not in East Sussex; she wanted to know what he'd got up to in Hyrule.

So as he played he took regular breaks to tell someone else about what he had encountered, and what he had discovered, and what he had yet to overcome. It is my bright delight today to be able to tell you that, around thirty years later, having just revisited the game in Nintendo's new remake, Ocarina of Time is still worth telling people about. It's still an adventure so surprising, so satisfying, so moving that I need to talk about it.

Here's the thing, though. Despite the game being a cast-iron masterpiece, this reaction was something of a surprise to me. Or rather, it was a surprise precisely because the game was a cast-iron masterpiece. In truth, a couple of weeks ago I wasn't really aching to head back to Ocarina of Time after all these years. I kind of dreaded having to slog through an echoing, once-great game and summon a thousand words of measured reverence afterwards.

Thankfully I had forgotten two things. The first is that I am an idiot, which means that I had failed to realise that any chance to replay a Zelda should be leapt at. And secondly, it turns out that I'd forgotten exactly what kind of masterpiece Ocarina of Time is. And along with that last point - and we'll get to this properly in a bit - I hadn't realised that there's a secret ingredient to this specific remake that has proved absolutely essential to its greatness, and that has nothing at all to do with the code running on the Switch 2.

Let's deal with remake duties here. This is more or less the game that people played back in 1998. The beats of the adventure, with Link travelling between different eras of Hyrule in order to defeat Ganon, remain the same. You still start in Kokiri Forest and you still ride Epona over the fence at Lon Lon Ranch in one of the great heart-racing, skin-prickling moments in all of art. If you're stuck in a dungeon, you can almost always use an existing guide from the N64 game to work your way through it. The game world is still broken up into little chunks that fade out and in as you move between them. And each game still begins with that unskippably evocative intro that has Link and Epona dashing over a misty hill with the moon behind them, Zelda meets Susan Cooper.

Changes though? For one thing the whole game's been updated with modern graphics, and has ditched any and all fixed cameras for a modern system of movement on one thumbstick and camera on the other. One example: Hyrule Castle Town, in my memory at least, always had a central camera and you would pivot about its streets as if you were a horse on a circus merry-go-round.

Now it's pure Frozen territory, cobbled paths racing off in every direction and you can explore them with the camera rushing behind you. The game's spaces feel less like elaborate machines that you were a mechanical part of, and more like video game spaces. Yes, from the way I've phrased that I now see that something of a trade-off has taken place here. I loved that sense you got in early 3D games that the whole thing was a clever practical trick that was forever almost about to collapse in on itself, but anyway! The new Ocarina is a game you can show kids who weren't alive in 1998 and they will feel at home more quickly.

Sometimes the graphical update is really wonderful. The route to Zora's Domain, which I barely remember in the original Ocarina, is now a lovely scramble over cliffs and arcing crags. A bold smattering of 1990s Nickelodeon colours and glossy textures, meanwhile, makes Jabu Jabu's Belly a cross between a pre-millennium rave and a modern soft play.

My favourite locations of all, however, are two of the mid-game dungeons. The Water Temple is now a drowned Art Nouveau palace that you could easily imagine Captain Nemo knocking around in. And the Forest Temple, which is my absolute favourite Zelda dungeon of all time, is a parquet-floored murder mansion that's been overtaken by trees. Dark wood-panelled corridors and weird bits of church sticking out of the foliage. It's the kind of place you'd hear a weird crime podcast about.

Trade-offs, however. My wife, a huge fan of the original game, would wander in and out as I played, and often felt the game she remembered was a little more characterful in its visuals. The moment you fight Dark Link, for example, has lost a little of its glitchy liminality, although the staging, with an old tree in the middle of a vast smoky room, is still wonderfully strange. Towards the end of the game, with the trip into Gerudo territory, you could also occasionally be forgiven for wondering if you'd stepped into one of a dozen third-person Unreal games in development at the moment. It's pleasant to look at, but it sometimes doesn't look specifically like Zelda anymore.

All that, and some strange character models that inevitably I learned to love, don't really matter very much, however. Not least because a handful of clever tweaks elsewhere mean that Ocarina now races along at an astonishing clip. And all this with a careful paring back of intrusions from Navi the fairy and the owl, who were both there in the original game to help move you along swiftly in the first place.

These tweaks are all pretty great. Link now has a jump button, which removes the original game's contextual jumps from the equation, and makes the thundering jump attack even more pleasing to pull off. Even better, in a loan from the 3DS remake of Ocarina, you can swap out usable items using a trigger mini-menu, and you can change boots and whatever you had on your head with some of the directional face buttons.

This doesn't sound like much, but it's quietly transformative in the way that the best updates are. Take the Water Temple. This is the stuff of memes in terms of difficulty and the patience required in navigating an environment that can be flooded up to three different levels. But now, as on the 3DS, you can enjoy it without having to slip into the menu every few steps to add your iron boots or remove them.

And this in turn means that a dungeon I had been really dreading turned out to be a dungeon that revealed itself via experimentation. I would change the water level and see what options I now had, think about the relationship between spaces I could reach and spaces I could not, all without needing to muddle about in menus and interrupt the flow of things. Flow is the word in fact: a simple UI and item-management tweak has given a legendary Zelda dungeon its perfect pace and sense of revelation.

Alongside tweaks like that which I noticed - oh, it's easier to equip the Hookshot - are no doubt hundreds of little things I didn't notice. I suspect there's a lot more gentle direction prompting built into places like the Water Temple, while elsewhere, in the Forest Temple, for example, I suspected that some of the backtracking had been removed. Individual item placements have been changed around perhaps, and there are generous tweaks like a wallet that holds more rupees from the off. For me at least, all of these tweaks had several things in common: they improved the game without removing or altering anything that was particularly meaningful about the original, and, also, I struggled to confidently spot them.

I did try to spot them for a while. And I measured up what I was playing against my memory, which is always wonky in the first place. Hyrule Field feels smaller now, but it's still thrilling to race across. I remember Epona entering the game sooner, or maybe I just remember the idea of a horse in a Zelda game being a huge, attention-holding deal. I thought a favourite boss involving oil paintings came much later, but again, I had just shifted it to the third act because of its original impact on me.

But this gives way over time to the most important thing about Ocarina of Time, I think, and the reason why this remake has such an emotional impact on people like me. This isn't just another Zelda with an ingenious overworld. It's a Zelda game that's even more preoccupied with time than ever before. And this is because time unfolds within the game, via a central mechanic that takes Hyrule seven years into the future, transforming Link from a child to an adult - and then allows you to go back again.

To play a game like this, a game that is about this stuff, after a period of almost thirty years away, gives your imagination and memory a kind of IMAX treatment. I realised that not only is this a game about the passage of time and the way that the world looks different when you're young to when you're a bit older - I had never really realised that adult Link goes through variations of the same dungeons that young Link went through, but now they're all just a bit harder and more consequential - it's also the kind of game that a lot of players have knowingly or unknowingly measured parts of their lives by.

This time, when I reached the first Fairy Fountain, for example, I remembered that my wife had walked down the aisle to this music - about half the registry office recognised the tune with a shiver of laughter, incidentally. Then there's the Water Temple, which has become cultural shorthand for a challenge that is really not screwing around. How many awful things had I faced in life by first conjuring the spirit of Link about to enter this place.

Players can change camera rotation speed and toggle motion-control aiming. Targeting can be controlled by holding a button or toggling a button, Ocarina can be controlled by the mic or by buttons, controller vibration can be switched on or off.

There's that first arrival in Hyrule Field, which is both a promise of everything that games were about to become in 1998 and that fleeting moment in childhood where the boundaries suddenly change. This game doesn't just send me back into my memories, then. It puts me back in conversation with all the people I have known who have ever loved it. I play it now and think I'm talking to my friend who played it at university, or my wife who loved it long before we met.

Was it always this good? Were the dungeons always so complex and challenging, but also so playful, so coherent? Were the bosses always driven by interesting ideas and drama and humour rather than difficulty? Was the world always so rich with minor characters who linger in the mind - the accordion player in the windmill, the gravedigger who has his own tour he'd really like you to try out? It's impossible for me to say at this point. This is Ocarina as it's always been, as timeless and intricate, but it also has a vibrancy and clarity that makes it feel like it was just created last Wednesday. If you're new to Zelda and you're coming to this game fresh, you're going to love it. Someone, somewhere, is about to play a game so special for the first time that they need to call up their friend and tell them all about it. But if you're returning to this game? Well, somehow you'll find that you're still coming to it fresh.

My dad always said that you should read Don Quixote three times in your life, and I only recently realised that he meant that the book changes because you change. So. When I got to the Deku Tree back at the start of Ocarina a few weeks ago, and I completed the dungeon and watched the impossible vision of the Deku Tree dying of weariness? I remembered that the first time I'd been here, almost thirty years ago, I couldn't help but identify with Link, freshly gifted a sword and shield, one dungeon down, ready for whatever came next. This time, I think, I was closer to identifying with the Deku Tree.

And here's the important part: this wasn't a depressing thought. It just meant that I had taken the long route to reach this point, and this astonishing game had taken the long route too. And yet we arrived at this point together, and together we were transformed. And that's really why any of this video game stuff matters.

A copy of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time was provided for this review by Nintendo.

#10857. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."
