#10855. Eight years ago the idea of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel had me captivated, but can Bethesda recapture the magic?

Eight years ago, I sat at my desk enthralled. Bethesda had just released the first trailer for The Elder Scrolls 6, a then mysteriously nameless, locationless sequel to one of the biggest games of all time, and I reckoned I'd just found the secrets to both. It was going to be set in the region of Hammerfell, I reckoned. And the city of Sentinel was a crucial factor in reasoning why.

Admittedly, I'm maybe a little more horrified at my level of Elder Scrolls obsession than I am chuffed at any apparent clairvoyance. Ultimately it was a bit of a punt, launched off the back of a day's worth of fanatical hyperfocus and exactly the right amount of the old Gamer Network office's weapons-grade filter coffee. "There's not really a lot to go on here", I wrote, sagely, "but, if we had to, we'd plant our flag in Hammerfell as the location of The Elder Scrolls 6."

Indulge me a bit and let's retread the reasoning. First, three assumptions were required in order to make any kind of guess at all: The Elder Scrolls 6 would not be set in all of Tamriel; not set outside of Tamriel; and would be set somewhere vaguely close in the timeline to the events of Morrowind, Oblivion and Skyrim - so as to make that bit of scrabbly rock and ruined castle, visible in that now infamous first teaser, at least somewhat traceable with contemporary descriptions in the lore.

Then came a lot of narrowing down. Not Morrowind, Cyrodiil, Skyrim or the Summerset Isles, I decided, as we'd been to all of those too recently (I'll leave it up to you whether another eight years since Morrowind lets that one still count as remotely "recent", but still). And then not Valenwood, Elsweyr, or Black Marsh, thanks to the topography of the land in the trailer and the type of environment on show. That left High Rock and Hammerfell, the former of which we ruled out because of its relative size - on the small side for a presumably beefy new TES game - and more importantly, the architecture that seemed at odds with the sandy stone just barely visible in the structures of that first teaser.

And finally: Sentinel. The city was perfectly positioned - along a coast with the sea to the right, dry mountains in the foreground, warm stone ruins to the left, no visible land beyond. Now, that setting is all but confirmed - "all but" as, technically, nothing's set in stone yet, pardon the pun. All we've since been shown is a sand dune, an Elder Scroll, the name Sentinel, and a brief quote from a Moth Priest to a Hammerfell High Queen. But we can pretty much lock it in.

To the Elder Scrolls obsessive who spent that day eight years ago scurrying down endless YouTube rabbit holes and lingering on the dustiest unofficial wiki pages, the thought of a game set there ought to be tantalising. Already, even with that paper thin new teaser, there's enough to start picking at. The new trailer's quote is dated just four years after the events of Skyrim, for instance, with both that territory and Cyrodiil located right next door, making for some lovely opportunities for crossovers and references to wider events. And the quote itself is curious. As our guides team have already speculated, the notion of "A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate" gives off the faintest whiff of a time-forking Dragon Break. That being the timeline-branching, multiple-player-choice-enabling thing that happened at the end of The Elder Scrolls 2: Daggerfall, a game which took place - aha! - in the Iliac Bay between High Rock and Hammerfell. Surely, the same bay we're looking at in that original TES 6 trailer.

It keeps going! The "victory" mentioned in that quote could refer to the victory won by the people of Hammerfell against a Thalmor invasion, in a conflict that - ooh! - took place around the same time Skyrim's main story was happening, and just a few years before that quote is dated. Could we get branching timelines? A tying together of the overarching Thalmor plotline that's been simmering away until now? A load of varying endings, as Bethesda experimented with so openly in Starfield? I'm off again. Fire up the Unofficial Elder Scrolls Pages!

There's such magic here, far beyond the apparent mastery Bethesda has over theory-baiting teasers for suckers like me. These games, for instance, also have an uncanny knack for joyful stupidity. There are Oblivion NPC barks out there that landed right as YouTube's golden era hit liftoff, and in turn defined an entire generation's sense of humour. I was once in a Slack channel here called #reeblereeblereeble, which was just myself and two other writers punting Imperial Watch taunts at each other to lighten the mood every couple of weeks, or whenever someone seemed a bit stressed. When I leave a gathering of old school friends I still catch myself bidding them "farewell!" with Wood Elf gusto, and giggling at the thought of being immediately squished into mulch.

And none of that is to mention the design itself, which has at times been more or less unparalleled. The clockwork, systemic magic of Morrowind is extraordinary, the first hour alone a lesson - at least as important as good old over-cited Mario 1:1 - in instructional design. Within a walkable radius of the musty little settlement of Seyda Neen Bethesda gave the player every tool necessary for understanding - and subsequently playing with, then outright breaking - its entire world. With Oblivion it brought the role-playing open world to the masses. With Skyrim it expanded what our very concept of the masses means.

By all accounts I should, then, be absolutely drooling at the thought of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. But for some reason the excitement is muted. It's been like this since long before this second teaser, frankly, and for much of that time I've put it down to Bethesda itself.

Bethesda Game Studios has not been on a good run. Fallout 76 might've finally recovered from its nightmarish launch, but time hasn't been hugely kind to Fallout 4, which longer-running fans have come to generally settle on as decent, but something of a slight step back from the wilder, more playful tendencies of Fallout 3 and Obsidian's New Vegas. The same could be argued of Skyrim, a wonderful game in its own right that did, nevertheless, sand down some of The Elder Scrolls' signature RPG elements into something smoother, almost closer to a frictionless modern action game (a topic on which Elder Scrolls fans have some rather strong opinions, though that's a whole other debate). And then there's Starfield, a perfectly alright game that, by virtue of that, is sort of a disaster. Its mere adequacy has only served to harden the belief, amongst long-running fans, that Bethesda has lost its magic touch.

The natural question, then, is can Bethesda recapture the magic? Again for some time I've been worried the answer might be 'no'. I worried about the BioWare-ification of the studio: the failed (albeit very well recovered) live service punt, the wonky new IP, the consolidation beneath changing owners, the shifting goalposts, the creeping corporate-ification and apparently inevitable entropy of big, creative RPG studios over a long enough period of time. The Bethesda of today is not the Bethesda of Morrowind, Oblivion or Skyrim. It's probably not even the Bethesda of Starfield. And worryingly, it's shown for some time now that it seems to care more for broad appeal - that mirage of financial success - over having caché with its long-running fans. That kind of blinkered thinking always comes back to bite.

But that's not it. I worried about that, but then I learned to stop worrying about that. The reality is Bethesda can absolutely recover the magic, just as any studio can - including BioWare for that matter, had it been given a clear, EA nonsense-free chance - for the obvious reason that no two development cycles need ever be the same. Development studios are not individuals, they're not sportspeople who go in or out of form, or see the talent fade as their legs start to give out. They're massive organisations full of all kinds of people. And so just as people leave, other people can join. There are always systemic issues at work, but you can overcome those if you can hire well, design well, plan well - if your owners are smart enough to let you, if you're given enough runway, and your leaders know what they're doing. Good game development, like just about anything done with a large team, is ultimately about making good decisions consistently over time. This is entirely possible for Bethesda to do, with the right people in charge to make them. That goes for the people above Bethesda's top brass, too.

Will Bethesda deliver? Yes, no, maybe. In a way it's besides the point. Instead, I've realised the thing that gives me pause, before burrowing giddily down into another warren of silly Tamriel backstory, is that the world this Elder Scrolls game will launch into will just be so thoroughly different to the last. Every video game from a western, triple-A publisher is life-or-death now. Every sales figure, metascore, or stray quote is another algorithmically driven hate-narrative, a collective Emperor's thumbs down. And every ultimate owner of these games now seems to be properly set on being really impossible not to loathe.

Bethesda can't make games that are just alright, because today alright means doom. It takes too long, for one - we're already well into GTA 6 territory here, 15 years on from Skyrim and counting, with master of whispers Jason Schreier suggesting at least another two to go. And by virtue of that, its games must surely cost too much. The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel will sell gangbusters, which will mitigate that. But at this level of spending, the level of the stratosphere, a sales hit alone arguably isn't enough. The world demands something more.

You take that long and cost that much and your game has to shake the mountains and rumble the earth. Move the industry or revive it. Sell physical consoles that are likely to cost into the low thousands. Rewrite a narrative of failure, incompetence, arrogance, waste, and decline. This is not me declaring those things necessary; it's the way of the industry now. The sands have shifted. A game like The Elder Scrolls 6 is now a symbol. In this case one of its owner, Xbox.

That company has no other cards to play but this one, as evidenced by CEO Asha Sharma jangling her Todd Howard-shaped keys every time the heat from all that delicious bloodletting gets a little much. Sentinel has to not only be successful, then, but widely adored. Anything else, after hundreds of millions on its development, billions on wider acquisitions, thousands upon thousands of layoffs (and, let's not forget, a boycott derived from still blood-splattered links to what a UN commission calls a genocide), will be a failure. Not a failure for Bethesda's developers, by any stretch, but for the company at large after the weight of context Xbox has dropped on top of them. A failure to flip over the table at the dying moment of a losing game. A failure for those who are all too desperate to change the story.

All this - all the perpetual, seemingly endless turmoil of making video games, the cynicism in the air of so many gaming spaces, the terminally compromised corporate ownership of the games themselves - is what makes excitement so hard to muster. I look at The Elder Scrolls now and can't help but feel a little tinge of anger or sadness. Anger that yet another route to open joy - and to unserious teen memories and, frankly, a tiny little part of a personal identity, no matter how hard I try to resist that - has become wound up with such unsavoury ownership. All this, reduced to yet another PR tool and distraction technique to shift eyes and words away from bleak and pointless cruelty. And then sadness, at the idea I might escape into it, whenever it finally arrives, only to find myself in exactly the world I wish I could just briefly leave behind. Bethesda's developers can recapture the magic. But can the world of video games let them?

#10854. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10852. Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10851. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10849. In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.

#10848. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10847. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

#10845. What we've been playing - "Sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Well, hello! I am once again wearing the Bertie hat in your usual host's absence, so joyous welcomes to our regular feature in which we talk about what we've been playing in recent times. This week, Marie has been revisiting Zelda - although perhaps not the one you might expect - while Dom is rediscovering Vice City of the 80s. Kelsey is playing some very weird cards, Connor is back in WoW - possibly forever - and I'm equally smitten with a different RPG.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. Bye!

Once again, I am finally playing a game from my backlog that I should've played years ago, and that's the delightful albeit dark card game Inscryption. There's so much more to this than you'd first expect, and I'm surprised the community has been able to keep the game's best bits under wraps for so long. Ultimately, nothing could've prepared me for Act 2, and my only real qualm with the whole experience is that I wish Act 2 had its own endless roguelike mode; you'd never be able to pull me away from it.

The game falters a little at the start of Act 3, I'll admit, and I've found myself very frustrated by what is an otherwise relatively easy time. This is down to me missing some rather useful earlier cards that I've since learnt would've made this final stretch much breezier, but oh well. I will still take down the Uberbots one way or another for the love of the game!

I've sunk every free hour I have into World of Warcraft: Forever since its release on Wednesday night. As of writing I am Level 19, waiting for an escort quest to come up in Westfall. So far things seem largely the same as in the original MMO, though the little changes and improvements I've seen have firmly planted a smile on my face.

This includes the new weather system. Like many others I was caught in a snow storm in Dun Morogh this week, a seemingly random environmental transformation that drastically limits view distance and blankets your screen with the cold stuff. It adds a lot, I think, to the fantasy of the game. I'll be playing this all weekend and for the foreseeable future.

I'll let you guess why I'm playing this right now of all times. Ahead of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch, I've made it my unofficial job to internalise the map of Vice City. I'm spending time driving around the strip, familiarising myself with the docklands, going for a few rounds of golf up in Leaf Links, and sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island. I'm immensely curious how similar the map is in GTA 6, and can't wait to see what's changed in the interceding 40 years in-universe. It's even better having V-Rock blast Iron Maiden and Twisted Sister at me; over the past few evenings, I've been playing this extra loud so it drowns out the sound of local fireworks for the sake of my poor, terrified dog. I don't think he's a fan of Bruce 'the air raid siren' Dickinson's voice - but it's better than all the apocalyptic banging.

The cold winter nights are drawing in, which calls for blankets, a warm drink, and a nice peaceful game before bed. I don't have an exact reason for going back to Tears of the Kingdom. Perhaps it's me diving into Ocarina of Time that fuelled my curiosity about returning, but what's surprised me the most is there's still so much I didn't see or do in my first 190 hours with it.

I started again, cleared my save file, and it turns out I've completely forgotten most things about the game - which is by no means a complaint! I'm rather enjoying making the little discoveries that are either brand-new to me, or ones that make me go 'AH-HA, I remember you' (in the case of Gloom Hands, the reaction was 'NOPE, NOPE, NOPE, NOPE.')

I've also had another surprise. I play in handheld mode mostly, and ToTK was all looking a bit blurry on the Switch 2's screen. So I got the upgrade pack and suddenly everything became beautiful again - Link was in focus, the individual blades of grass in Hyrule Field were clearly whipping in the wind, and the crispy snow around Rito village almost sparkled. I don't often get upgrade packs for games I already own, but this one was worth it.

I suspect this won't be the last time you see me yapping about Queen's Domain here. I've already enthused about it elsewhere on the site this week, but I've managed to squeeze in a few more hours since then, and it continues to enthrall. Here's a small moment from my most recent go: I'd been fumbling about the Tower of Knowledge for a while at this point, dying slightly more than I'd like to those jabby gargoyle gits and lumbering chimera things, so I thought I'd have a little wander away from the violence and have an explore. That was when I noticed the gap on the bookshelf, wide enough to slot in the mysterious tome - the Ratty Bug Compendium - I'd been wondering about ever since I picked it up elsewhere some five hours earlier.

Moments later a secret passage had opened, leading out to a balcony overlooking the ocean. Treasure! Exciting! But just as I'm about to go back inside, I notice a little outcrop on the exterior of the tower, seemingly reachable with my sword's magic dashing power. So over I go, discovering an otherwise inaccessible route into an inner chamber. There, I find a lever, which I excitedly pull, only to be rewarded with... nothing at all. Until five minutes or so later, when I discover a secret panel has now opened up on the other side of the tower, granting me access to the treats within. In isolation, it's not a remarkable gaming anecdote, but Queen's Domain is just crammed with these little incremental secrets and surprises, full of clever design that makes exploration feel so rewarding. I've already spotted more enigmas waiting to be cracked, and if Queen's Domain keeps distracting me like this, I might never escape its pull.

#10844. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.

#10843. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10841. The first Snapdragon phone with Android Linux Terminal support is here

The Redmagic 12 Pro is the first Snapdragon-based Android phone to support the Linux Terminal introduced in Android 16 for Pixel phones.

Android power users who want a new Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6-powered phone like the Redmagic 12 Pro now have another reason to be excited about the latest Qualcomm processors: the Android Linux Terminal.

Thanks to tipster Dylan H, who wrote in asking about compatibility. After enabling the Android Linux Terminal and running a few commands to check compatibility, I found out that the Redmagic 12 Pro does, indeed, support the Linux Terminal app. I’ve written Qualcomm asking about wider Snapdragon support for Linux Terminal and will update this article if I receive a response.

Google introduced the Terminal app as a power user feature in the Android 16 update for Pixel phones, and Samsung added support in One UI 8.5 for Exynos-powered Galaxy S26 models. So far, however, Snapdragon phones have not appeared on the compatibility list.

this is super cool! i guess my first question is if it is subject to the Android 32 subprocess limit that effectively cripples the terminal on Googlebooks.

Linux Terminal support has been slowly building on Google platforms. Googlebooks sort of support it, and now it appears that more Android phones will support it as well. The implementation on the Redmagic 12 Pro seems slightly different from what’s on Exynos-powered Samsung phones, as you cannot adjust the disk size (it’s locked at 132GB available), but you can adjust the amount of memory available for the terminal to use.

By default, Terminal has access to 1GB of memory, although the Redmagic 12 Pro lets you increase that to 10.6GB. You can also adjust the display resolution and enable Keep Awake in advanced settings. The Redmagic 12 Pro also supports the GUI feature, so programs like GIMP should work if you can get them installed (I kept running into 404 errors using the standard installation command).

The Redmagic 12 Pro goes up for preorder on November 12 and includes several freebies and a launch discount before November 17. See our full review to find out if it’s right for you.
